R160 has one carve-out, sign-up's own 429 for the mail interval, named at the end of this row. The API states the wait as `Retry-After` and `details.retryAfterMs`. For a redemption refused at step 2 or 3 the wait is the whole attempt window, an upper bound, because the exact time would depend on the IP window and so on other accounts' attempts. The code screen's status does say exactly when this account's own tries come back (its oldest counted attempt leaving the window), and when a paused breaker reopens, and the screen reads the status again then, so neither state waits for a reload. Every wait the screen states is a deadline on the clock: its sentence counts down, and a page shown again after it passed acts at once, since a sleeping tab's timers are frozen. A redemption refused because redemption is paused (a 503, which the database's own switch answers too) turns Redeem off at once, and the server keeps saying paused in its status from then on, so a player does not spend tries on a paused service. Two provider requests are an exception. Resending a confirmation and asking for a password reset answer the same neutral sentence for every provider answer except an unreachable provider and an invalid address, because the provider answers an unknown address without ever reaching its mailer: a rate limit (its per-address interval), a failed send or an allow-list refusal can only happen to an address that has an account. Instead, the client waits out the provider's per-address interval (`AUTH_EMAIL_RESEND_COOLDOWN_SECONDS`) for the address it mailed, a sign-up included, before it offers that address the button again. The interval is counted on the clock from the send this browser remembers, so a reload or a tab the browser put to sleep neither restarts nor stalls it; a send this browser does not remember (another device's) starts nothing here. The neutral sentence says, for every address alike, that a send asked for inside the interval (from any device) cannot go out yet, and the wait beside the button says only how long, never that a mail went out. Sign-up's own 429 for the mail interval is the one provider signal left visible, and so R160's one carve-out: the provider gives it to any direct caller whatever the client says, it only tells that an unconfirmed sign-up for the address was mailed in the last interval, and the same answer is the project's overall mail limit, which a new player must be told about rather than sent to an empty inbox | §9.4, §9.8, R109, R145, R160 |
| R193 | What an emailed auth link may do | A confirmation or recovery link carries its tokens in the URL fragment (one mailed since R323 carries a one-time code instead, which `/login` exchanges for the same session, and this row then judges that session). The client reads them once and removes them from the address bar and history before anything renders, on whatever path the link lands (the provider falls back to the project's Site URL), and the link is then handled on the sign-in screen. It then goes to a fixed route, never to a URL found in a query string or fragment. The provider's own error text is never shown: a known error code maps to the client's own sentence. A **confirmation** link never signs this browser in, not even for the sign-up this browser started: the email is confirmed (the provider did that when the link was opened), the session the browser already has is untouched, and the player is asked to sign in with their password. Accepting a token from a link would let an attacker sign a victim into the attacker's pending account, where the victim's own invite code would activate the attacker. Nor is a link proof of the password: the provider leaves an existing unconfirmed account unchanged when the same address signs up again, so an address someone else registered first keeps that person's password through the owner's own sign-up and confirmation, and a session taken from the link would hide it for as long as renewal lasts. Signing in by hand exposes it: the owner's password is refused, and the reset that follows replaces the other one. A **recovery** link is held (for the reset screen, never as the stored session) at once if its address matches a reset this browser asked for. Otherwise (a reset asked for on another device or in another browser, which is the common case) nothing is held until the player types their account's address, and the link is held only if that matches its checked address. Someone sent another person's link types their own address, which does not match, so they are not signed into that account, and the session this browser has is untouched; the owner on a second device gets through. Leaving that question revokes the link. The guard is only as good as the address it compares, so nothing from a link is ever shown or filled into a form, not even its checked address: a mailer form holding an address the player did not type would arm the guard with it at one click. Nor is anything filled in from storage: `/login?mode=forgot` is a public link, and the reset address a shared computer remembers may be another person's. A link's session is renewed once as soon as it is read, before the server is asked whose it is, because its tokens were in a URL that the browser's history keeps: the refresh token that URL carried is then spent whatever the tab does next (closing it while the link is being checked included), and everything after (the check, holding, revoking) uses the renewal. A renewal the provider refuses means the link was opened before, and nothing is held. A link's session that is not held (every confirmation, a reset whose address was not typed, a dashboard invite, a confirmation that could not be checked, one the player moved on from) is revoked, renewed first if its access token has run out (R194). A recovery link the game server could not check is kept on the sign-in screen, and only there, so it can be checked again (it works only once), and is revoked when the player moves on. The sign-in screen says when the browser already holds a session, and whose, because signing in replaces and revokes it. The recovery session lives in this tab only, in memory and mirrored to the tab's `sessionStorage` so a reload does not spend the reset, and never in `localStorage`. It becomes the stored session only once a new password is saved, and the reset screen says so when that will sign this browser out of another account. Leaving the reset screen unsaved abandons it (forgets and revokes it), but its own exits ask first, because the link works only once | §9.2, §9.4, §9.8, R160 |
| R194 | When a session is renewed and when it ends | An access token within `AUTH_SESSION_REFRESH_MARGIN_SECONDS` of expiring is renewed with the session's refresh token before it is used, and while a screen is open the gate renews that far ahead of the expiry of the token it handed down and hands down the new one, so a screen's own requests (a queue wait, a deckbuilder save) never carry an expired token. A renewal of the same session in another tab does not replace a token that still has longer than the margin to live, so nothing keyed on it (a match socket) is reopened for nothing. A token the API refuses as unauthorised when the gate or the code screen made the call is renewed once, and the request is retried. A session's expiry is counted on this device's clock, from the lifetime the provider states (`expires_in`), never from its absolute `expires_at`, so a device whose clock is off does not read a fresh session as expired. Concurrent renewals share one provider call, because the provider rotates refresh tokens and treats a reused one as stolen. A renewal the provider refuses ends the session, and the sign-in screen says so rather than failing silently. Signing out revokes the session at the provider as well as clearing the device, so a copied token cannot outlive the sign-out. The API honours an access token only while the provider still has its session: a token that names its session is checked with the provider, and only a live answer is remembered, for `AUTH_SESSION_LIVE_CACHE_SECONDS`, so a session ended by a sign-out, a revoked link or a password reset stops working at the API within that time rather than when its access token expires. A provider that cannot be reached signs nobody out (sign-in is down with it), and the email then counts as R159 says. The provider refuses to revoke with an expired access token, so an expired session is renewed first and then revoked (a signed-out session, and an abandoned recovery session or link, alike), with sign-out waiting at most `AUTH_SIGN_OUT_WAIT_SECONDS` for the two, and saying so; a second press meanwhile joins the first rather than leaving before the revocation is sent. A live one is revoked without waiting. A session belongs to its tab (R632), so a sign-out in another tab ends only that tab's; what the tabs still share (the end-to-end fixtures' `localStorage` session, and one an older build left there) is watched, and its removal in another tab is noticed. A session with no refresh token (the end-to-end fixtures) is never renewed. A recovery session held for the reset screen (R193) is renewed the same way before the new password is sent, and once more if the provider refuses its access token, so a slow player is not told the link was spent; only a renewal the provider refuses means it was | §9.2, §9.4 |
| R195 | When a card glows yellow (`conditionActive`) | Hearthstone lights a playable card yellow when its special condition is met; §10.8 did not say whether the view may carry that. It does, as `conditionActive: true` on the **viewer's own** cards only, computed by the engine and never by the client (CLAUDE.md rule 7). A card whose script declares `conditionMet` (§10.9) is asked with `zone: "hand"` for a card in the viewer's hand, and only during the viewer's own main phase with no prompt open and no result, the only time it could be played. It is asked with `zone: "field"` for a unit on top of its pile or a backrow card the viewer controls. The flag is absent otherwise (never `false`) and never set on the opponent's cards. A hook reads only what its controller may see (§9.1), so it reveals nothing. A hook is a pure read (no writes, no random draws) and must agree with the branch the card's own resolution would take if it resolved now. The Core cards with a printed condition implement it: #10 (Combo 3: three or more cards played earlier this turn, in hand), #53 (your hero below 30, radiant 60, in hand), #68 (your hero below 10, in hand), #71 (your library larger than the opponent's, in hand and on the field) and #93 (on the field, during its controller's turn, when the cards played reach its grade and it is not at S). Scaling amounts (#70), conditions a player modifier grants to other cards (#38, #64, #78), trap triggers that wait for an event, and cost reductions (#100) are not conditions for this flag | §10.8, §10.9, #10, #53, #68, #71, #93, C #22, C #36, C #40 |
| R196 | How a fused card glows yellow | R77 fuses two or three cards into one definition whose Cry, Death and trigger lists run every ingredient's in turn, so each ingredient's printed condition still picks its own branch when the fused card resolves; R195 did not say whether such a card glows. It does when **any** ingredient's condition holds: the fused script's `conditionMet` asks each ingredient's hook with the fused card's own context (the fused instance as `self`, the face it runs, the zone it is asked about) and answers `true` if one of them answers exactly `true`. An ingredient with no hook adds nothing, so one hooked ingredient's hook is the fusion's unchanged and a fusion with none never glows. The hook is or-ed rather than concatenated like the list hooks because it answers a boolean (§10.9), as `setStat` is summed rather than concatenated (R102). Or, not and: the glow says that playing or keeping the card now takes a branch its text makes conditional, and one ingredient's branch is enough for that. Each hook is asked about the board as it stands, while at resolution a later ingredient's list reads the board the earlier ones left (R102), so a Reno + Twisted Sorcerer played with its hero at 5 glows, and Reno's half sets the hero to 30 before the Sorcerer's half reads it and deals 4 | §6.3, §10.9, R77, R102, R195 |
| R200 | The effects layer paces nothing | §10.10's table owns every duration the client waits on, and the effects layer (`apps/web/src/fx`) only decorates it. An effect starts with the table entry that carries its event. Anything it times, such as a projectile's arrival, an impact or a splat, lands inside that entry's duration, and whatever trails after it (particles, splats, rays, cracks, the board shake) is gone within `FX_MAX_TAIL_MS` of the entry's end, counted in real time even when frames stall. The layer's own elements never carry a `data-animating` of their own (a stand-in's copy echoes the one the board puts on the card it carries and drops it in the same task, so `cy.settled()` waits exactly as long), never take a pointer event and never delay the view swap, so a burst settles exactly when it would without effects and no click is blocked by one. The stage effects act on the board's own elements instead, and are bounded by the view swap rather than the tail: a stand-in that carries a moved card to its new zone and a card hidden because the burst has taken it away both last until the board shows the next view, which only the runner decides, and never longer than `FX_HOLD_MAX_MS`. A hidden card takes no pointer event, like any card mid-animation, since the view the board is about to show no longer has it. An aimed lunge ends with its entry and is never shorter than BUILD's lunge distance. Under `prefers-reduced-motion`, or the viewer's reduce setting, no effect is drawn at all (the Divine Shield cocoon included), and the setting stops the table's CSS motion exactly as the media query does. The game-over sequence and the hot-seat hand-over banner run off the shown view rather than an entry. The game-over sequence first replays the killing blow that the finished view's drain cut short, in at most `FX_LETHAL_LEAD_MAX_MS`, then runs within `FX_RESULT_MS`; the banner has its own constant; neither holds anything either | §10.10, BUILD M5-T4, BUILD M8 (`cy.settled()`) |
| R201 | The effects-speed setting scales the animation table | The viewer's effects speed `s` runs from `FX_SPEED_MIN` 0.25 to `FX_SPEED_MAX` 3 (0.5 to 2 before patch v0.2.0, R435), set by a slider, and defaults to 1. It divides every non-zero BUILD M5-T4 duration, never below `MIN_ENTRY_MS`, and divides the burst budget the same way. At `s = 1` the durations are exactly BUILD's. The CSS keyframes follow the duration the runner actually gives an entry (`--anim-squeeze`, covering both the speed and the burst budget), so a shortened entry plays its whole motion instead of being cut off. It is a per-viewer presentation setting: nothing in the engine, the view or the event stream changes, and the reduce setting and `prefers-reduced-motion` still collapse every duration to 0 | §10.10, BUILD M5-T4, R435 |
| R202 | Effects draw from the redacted stream only | An effect is chosen from the event, the view the runner planned it against and the public catalog, and from nothing else, so it can never say more than §10.8 lets the viewer read. A card moving out of or into a hidden zone is drawn as a back. An id or `defId` redacted to `"hidden"` gets the same effect whatever card it hides. The rarity entrance plays only for a unit whose `defId` the viewer can read as it is summoned. A face-down trap's reveal is anchored to its zone (R154) and looks the same for every trap until the view names the card | §10.8, §10.10, R97, R154 |
| R203 | What audio may reveal | Sound reads the viewer's `PlayerView` and nothing else, so it follows R97's and R154's redaction exactly: an event whose card is the sentinel plays a generic sound and never that card's voice line. Setting a Trap never speaks on either seat, because a line on the set would name a face-down card to anyone within earshot of the device: its owner hears the set sound every Trap shares, and the other seat, whose event carries the sentinel, hears the plain play sound of any card it cannot name. A Trap speaks when it fires, and only on its controller's seat, because R154 withholds its identity from the other seat at that moment. A hotseat hand-over plays nothing the arriving seat's view did not produce. A sound may vary with the public catalog's facts about a card the viewer can read (its type, tags and rarity): a Unit's arrival on the field and a cast Spell do, a card behind the sentinel never does, and a Trap never does, so its set and its arrival in the backrow sound like every Trap's | #18, #41, #60, #71, #85, #96; §10.8, §10.11, R33, R97, R154, R202 |
| R204 | Which moments speak | A Unit speaks its play line on its `cardPlayed`, whether played or cast by an effect (the plays R1 fires a Cry on), and whether or not the Cry then resolves (a trap can take the card off the field first, R174); a countered card is never played and has no `cardPlayed` (§10.5). A Unit an effect puts onto the field without playing it (a token, a copy, Recruit or Reborn) speaks that line on its `summoned` instead, at the lowest priority; a played Unit's own `summoned` adds nothing, and a Transform speaks nothing. It speaks its death line on its `destroyed`, whose `defId` R89 carries, tokens included, and never on a bounce, an exile or a Transform, which are not deaths. A Spell or Field Spell speaks its cast line on its `cardPlayed`, casts included (R70). A Trap or Field Trap speaks on its `trapFired`, per R203. One line speaks at a time. A death line or a firing trap's line cuts in on a play or cast line, because it answers something that has just happened: that is how R17's play-reactive traps (#41, #60, #85), whose `trapFired` comes in the same action as the play it answers, are heard over that play's line. Any other line waits briefly behind the one speaking and is dropped if it has waited too long. A fused transient definition (R77) has no lines | every card; §10.11, R1, R17, R70, R77, R89, R174, R203 |
| R209 | A permanent's lasting effect lasts while it is on the field | A lasting effect a permanent installs on its player — #79 Twinspell's "the next Spell you play gains Echo +1", which §5.1's "a permanent in the backrow with a lasting effect" describes — belongs to that permanent's stay on the field. It is the permanent's and not a Cry's (#79 prints no "Cry:", R169), so a Twinspell has it however it came to stand on the field — played, summoned (#22's copies, #95's backrow, #98's recruit), or carried onto another permanent by a Fuse (#85, R77), whose controller it then serves (§8 Conventions). It ends the moment the permanent leaves, however it leaves (destroyed by #36 or #88, bounced by #52's radiant face or a Locked rotation, exiled, eaten by #22, replaced by #83, fused away by #85), and a Twinspell that comes back installs its own, so one Twinspell on the field grants one Echo (R174). While it stands the effect follows the permanent: to its new controller when control changes (R171, §8 Conventions), and to its current face, so a Twinspell made Radiant on the field (#49 radiant) grants Echo +2 from then on without its Cry firing again (§5.2). R30's consumption is unchanged: the Spell that takes the grant sends that Twinspell to its owner's graveyard. Hearthstone ends a minion's or a location's effect when it leaves play | #36, #49, #52, #79, #83, #85 |
| R210 | The zone a play names is held for it | §10.5 step 1 accepts the zone a play names and step 4 puts the card in it, and between them step 2 pays the Tribute — whose units die (§6.3), so a Death can land cards in the very row the play is going to: #3 radiant's base copy and #22's copies by R64, #86's steals by R15. The named zone is held for the play from step 2 to step 4 exactly as a zone is held for a dying Reborn unit (R64), so those cards take another zone and the played card lands where the player put it; a played card is never left in no zone (§10.1). A tributed unit with Reborn comes back to its own zone at 1 health like any other first death (§6.1, §4.5 step 4), since a sacrifice counts as a death | #3, #22, #55, #66, #86 |
| R211 | Concede is always on offer | A player may concede at any moment of a live game (§2.5), and `reduce` accepts a concede from either seat while a prompt is open (BUILD M1-T3), so `legalActions` offers it to both seats then too: the prompt's holder is offered its answers and concede, the other seat concede alone. §10.7's policy draws from `legalActions` minus R84's skipped actions, concede among them, so the AI policy and a timeout (R79) still answer only the prompt. Hearthstone offers the concede button at any time | §2.5, §10.2, §10.7, R79, R84 |
| R212 | An event is answered as the board stood when it happened | §10.3's loop hands an event to the triggers some time after it happened — after the state check that follows a combat, an Echo repeat or a whole Cry (§4.5), and inside that check after the Death hooks and Reborn — so the board it is offered to can have moved on. The cards that answer it are the ones that stood where they are now when it happened: a card that has moved zones since — returned through Reborn, drawn or put in a hand, played or summoned — is on a stay that did not see the event and does not answer it (R174), so #91's Plague Token for the hit that killed it never lands on its Reborn body, a #32 Prem Panther that traded and came back through Reborn draws nothing for the kill of its last stay, and a #89 Corpse Eater drawn after a death does not feed on it. A card whose controller has changed since answers for the player who controlled it when the event happened, and a change of control is not a move (R171): a #32 that attacks and kills #86 "Miss" Mrow draws for its controller (R426), even though Mrow's Death steals it before the draw resolves. The events that happened after the one being dispatched are what say so — those still owed behind it, and those another loop has already dispatched, #96's AI turn among them: a #89 drawn during the AI turn My Pawn hands over does not feed on a death a trap dealt earlier in the same window. A card that resumes as its Stack pile's top when the card above it leaves (§3.2) lay dormant until then and registered nothing (R153): it does not answer the event whose removal of the top uncovered it — the top's death, bounce, exile or steal — nor any event before it, as a Reborn body does not answer the hit that killed its unit; no event reports a resume, so the engine keeps it against the card that left, for that removal alone: a later move of the card that left (exiled out of the graveyard it died into, discarded, shuffled back) uncovers nothing, and the card that resumed long before answers what happened before it. Traps are its first responders and answer the same way (§10.3): a trap that reached the field after the event — summoned by the rest of the list whose draw cast the card — is not offered it, and one whose controller has changed since answers for the player who controlled it then, whose tokens it makes (R52), in an immediate dispatch and in the end-of-turn window alike. Hearthstone makes a Reborn minion a new entity, and resolves a minion's kill trigger for its controller before a Deathrattle | #32, #86, #89, #91, any granted Reborn (R21); R426 |
| R213 | Gifted Program counts its controller's plays | #64's "the first (1) Cost or less card you play each turn" counts the player's plays that turn — every card they played and what each actually paid (R56), a cast's 0 included (R70) — and not a flag on the Field Spell. So a card costing 1 or less played before a Gifted Program arrived that turn was already the first; a Gifted Program that fired for its owner and is then stolen (#36 radiant, #49), swapped (#87) or rotated across (#52) has not spent its new controller's first cheap card, nor its old controller's on the way back; and one that leaves and comes back (R174) does not give its player a second. Each Gifted Program asks with its own face's threshold (2 or less on the radiant one), and only the ones on the playing player's side of the field count (§8 Conventions). The turn log records what each play paid (`costsPaid`), and the engine reads the card as a static flag at §10.5 step 3 (R214). Hearthstone's Pint-Sized Summoner counts the minions its player played that turn the same way | #36 radiant, #49, #52, #64, #87 |
| R214 | A play's choices are its resolving face's | §10.5 step 3 can make the played card Radiant as it is played (#64), after step 1 has checked the play's targets and modes and before step 5 resolves them, and the Radiant face can declare other choices: #87's "you may skip adding it", #48's "all enemy units, or all units", a crafted card whose radiant Bigot names no target (R102). The targets and modes a play carries (R81) are therefore the choices of the face step 5 will resolve — which step 1 already knows, because the cost the play pays is what decides it (R56, R213) — and step 1 checks them, and `legalActions` offers them, against that face (R90). The Tribute is paid at step 2, before the face changes, so it is the face in hand's | #48, #64, #87, #99 |
| R215 | A card that reaches a graveyard or exile from a hand or a library is the printed card again | R78 resets a card that leaves the field, and a hand or library card that reaches a graveyard or an exile pile is reset the same way — discarded (#80), burned by a full hand (§2.4), put there with a replaced hand (#76) or a full library (R80), exiled (#40, #78, #94) — keeping only what R78 keeps in every zone: `costMod`, `costOverride`, `radiant`, `tuning`, the Brittle count and the times-played count. So a #89 Corpse Eater that fed in hand and was discarded comes back by #72 Reminisce as the printed 2/2, and a #98 Heroic Power returned from a graveyard rolls its power again (R151). A card that lands from the resolving zone at §10.5 step 7 is reset the same way, its play being over: a #95 Call to Chaos an earlier one cast (R87) carries no link of that chain (R28) into a play of its own once #72 or #37 has brought it back. A price a card is given as it reaches a hand — #31's +1, #37r's and #72's 1 less, #72r's and radiant #52's 0, #54's 1 or 0, #95's 0, #99's crafted 0 — is its price in that hand, so a card a full hand burns instead reaches the graveyard without it. Hearthstone returns a card from the graveyard without the enchantments it had in hand | #31, #37, #52, #54, #72, #76, #80, #89, #95, #98, #99; R78 |
| R216 | Nothing happens after the game is over | The state check that finds a hero at 0 or less ends the game at once (§2.5, §4.5 step 2), and nothing still to resolve then resolves: the rest of the effect list the check ran inside (#5 Stockpile's "heal your hero 2" after its draw cast a CN-Virus that killed its own hero, #74's summon after its draw), owed work, queued triggers, or a trap's consumption after the AI turn it handed over ended the game (#96). R96 already stops a forced run this way. `gameOver` is the last event of the action that ended the game, and a question still open when it ends — a Discover the other seat conceded under (R211) — is closed with it, since nothing can answer it. Hearthstone ends the game at the death phase that finds a hero dead and resolves nothing after it | #5, #74, #90.1, #96, R59, R96 |
| R217 | A draw a cast makes continues its chain | R58 caps the cast-on-draw chain one draw sets off at 20 casts (`CAST_ON_DRAW_CHAIN_CAP`), and a cast can draw: radiant /fullsend's "Combo: draw 1" rides on every card played that turn, a cast included (R70), and a cast Spell's own text can draw. A draw a cast-on-draw cast makes is part of that cast, so it continues the chain's count instead of beginning a chain of its own, and the cap bounds everything the first draw set off, however deeply its casts draw: once the chain has cast 20 cards, the next cast-on-draw card any draw of it meets goes to the hand uncast. The draw that began the chain ends it, when its own repeat is done. Every other draw — a played card's, a trigger's, each of "draw N"'s N — begins a chain of its own, as R58 says. Without this a CN-Virus cast under /fullsend drew the next CN-Virus before its script shuffled two more in, and the nested chains never ended. Hearthstone has no cast-on-draw loop to bound, and this keeps the designer's cap whole | #21, #78, #90.1, R58, R70 |
| R218 | A Recruit passes over a unit-token card | A unit-token card in a library — one of the copies #33 shuffles in when the Rush Token card #75 gave is played (R34) — leaves it only by being drawn (R11), so a Recruit (#69 Call to Arms, #71 Intern Stimmy, #98's Recruit power) passes over it to the next card its filter matches, as it passes over a Spell (§6.3), and finds nothing when no other card matches. Summoning it would take it out of the library some other way, and R11 would have it cease to exist on the way The same holds for a reveal from the library that puts the pick in a hand (#51 KY's Private Tutor): it passes over a unit-token card, and the Tutor offers no type or bracket that only such a card matches. | #33, #69, #71, #75, #98 |
| R219 | A gain is never a loss | A card that gains another card's numbers gains nothing below 0. #89 Corpse Eater gains the dying unit's attack and max health (R38), read off the `destroyed` event (R89), and a unit #46 Suppressive Aura starved below 0 max health (R69) reports that number: the Eater gains 0 from it rather than shrinking, on either face. §10.4 floors attack at 0 already. Hearthstone's "gain its stats" never lowers a stat either | #46, #89 |
| R220 | A declared attack resolves only as it was declared | §4.2 step 4's window stands between a declaration and its combat, and a trap in it can change the attack: destroy or bounce the attacker or its target, steal either, swap the boards. The attack is aimed at the stays that were declared (R174), so step 5 resolves the combat only while both still stand as declared: the attacker on the field on the stay that declared it and still its declarer's, and the target on the field on its stay and still the attacker's enemy (§4.2 step 2, R173). An attacker or a target that has left the field is gone even when a Reborn body is back in its zone: that is a new arrival (R83), which declared nothing and was declared on by nobody. Otherwise the attack is over, like one My Pawn cancelled: no combat, and the exertion stays spent (R44), unless the attacker changed sides, whose exertion R171 resets for its new controller. A trap later in the same window meets no attack either, so a My Pawn after the trap that ended it stays armed and face-down (R99). Hearthstone cancels an attack whose attacker or defender has left play | #96, R44, R171, R173, R174 |
| R221 | A prompt's answer is a set | The picks of an answer are the options it names, taken in the order the prompt offered them, whatever order the answer lists them in. R60 makes N picks N different cards, `legalActions` offers each set once, in that order (§10.2), and `reduce` accepts any listing of it, so a listing must never mean something no offered answer does: #80 Zao Gao discarded its picks in turn until R354 made its discard random, and the order of a graveyard is public; a radiant #26's Echo repeat picks two the same way. The same holds for one declaration's picks in a play: they are taken in the order the declaration offers its options, which is the order `legalActions` lists them in (R90), whatever order the play lists them in | #80, R16, R60 |
| R222 | Zephyrs' dry run reads only what its player may read | §10.7's dry run plays each candidate on a copy of the state, and the three cards #97 offers must not tell the caster anything §9.1 hides from them. So on that copy every card the caster may not read — the opponent's hand, both libraries (the caster's own too, §3) and a face-down trap the caster does not control (R33) — stands in its place as a card with no text, which answers nothing, casts nothing when drawn and fires on nothing; every count the caster can see is unchanged. Played on the real cards, a Sheepish turned every Unit candidate into a Sheep, and a cast-on-draw card on top of the caster's library hurt the caster, so the offer said which trap it was and what lay on top. Hearthstone's Zephrys ignores the opponent's Secrets and does not know the order of the deck | #97, R29, R33, R427 |
| R223 | An instance id says nothing about the deck it came from | The numbers a deck's cards take when the game is created (`createGame`) are handed out in an order drawn from the match seed's own stream, not in the order the deck was handed over in. The store returns a deck sorted by card id, so numbered in list order a public card's id was its rank in its owner's sorted deck, and told the opponent how many of that deck's cards — hidden ones included — sort before it (§9.1, R97). The library is still built in the handed-over order for §2.1's shuffle, and the numbering stream is not the match's rng, whose draws are unchanged. Like the library's order, the numbering is known only to the seed. The same holds for a batch of cards made mid-game in a library: #83 Transmogulate's replacements take their new numbers in an order drawn from that stream, not the library's top-down walk, so the first number of the next public card does not tell its owner where a library card they are later shown lies (§10.8). An id seen while its card was public is a different matter, and R227 settles it: a card set face-down takes a fresh id | §2.1, §9.1, R97, R177 |
| R224 | Setup waits for a question | §2.1's opening draw and R9's replacement draws are draws, and a draw can ask: a card's start-of-game clause runs as the card arrives in a hand (R151), and its question is its owner's (R81). (A cast-on-draw card, whose cast asked, was the other source until R635 set those cards aside; setup draws none now.) That question is state until it is answered (§9.3), and §10.1 allows one prompt at a time, so setup does not open the mulligans over it, or go on resolving them: the draw owes its own remainder (R158) and setup owes the rest of itself — the other seats' opening draws and the mulligans, or the returned cards' shuffle-back, the seats still to resolve and the game (R265) — which the answer's drain finishes (R122). The returned cards wait with that remainder, in no pile, until they go back, and are read meanwhile as the library cards they are about to be: nobody reads them (§3, §9.1), so the events of the opening deal that name them stay redacted in the other seat's view, as they are once the cards are back. The mulligan's answer names the prompt it answers, as every other answer does (§10.6) | §2.1, §2.4, R9, R81, R151, R158, R635 |
| R225 | A Quickdraw card is one of the opening draws | §2.1's "any Quickdraw card in a deck replaces one of these draws" and §6.2's "instead of a draw" are read literally: each Quickdraw card replaces one of its seat's opening draws, the last ones, and no seat is dealt more of them than it has draws (R640). The seat draws its other opening cards first, off a library whose dealt Quickdraw cards wait at the bottom, and then each goes to the hand as the draw it replaces — reported as a draw (`drawn`, then `addedToHand`) and counted as one by #100's draw counter (R55). So nothing the other seat sees says whether the opening hand holds one (§9.1): not #100's price or whether it can be paid, not the deal's events (R97 keeps a redacted event's type), and not the hand and library counts while a question the opening draw made is being asked (R224). Hearthstone has no Quickdraw; this keeps the rule's own words | §2.1, §6.2, R55, R97, R224, R640, #65, #84, #98, #100 |
| R226 | A card that leaves the hand before it is placed is not played | §10.5 step 4 moves the played card out of its owner's hand, and until then it is still there, so a Tribute's Death at step 2 or an `onPlayHook` at step 3 can move it first — a discard whose question offers it, a random or whole-hand discard. A card that is no longer in its owner's hand at step 4 stays where that move put it, in one zone (§10.1), and is not played: it is not placed, no `cardPlayed` goes out, it is not counted as a play and nothing of it resolves. What steps 2 and 3 did stands — the mana is spent and the Tribute paid — the zone step 2 held is released (R210), and step 8 settles it. A cast is a play (R70), and its card waits in the resolving zone from the start of the cast rather than in a hand: one a step-3 `onPlayHook`, or that hook's answer, has moved out of it — exiled it — stays where that move put it and is not played, and is never pulled back out of exile to be placed. Hearthstone takes a card out of the hand as it is played, so nothing could discard it mid-play; this engine holds it in the hand until step 4, and this is what that costs. The Hearthstone reading was weighed and not taken. SPEC is not silent on where the card is: §10.5 step 4 is the step that moves it out of the hand, so through steps 2 and 3 it is a hand card for every reader, and the Hearthstone default is for a decision SPEC does not make; this row decides only what step 4 does when the card has already gone. Keeping it in the hand but out of what hand-reading effects see would give the hand two sizes, the one the other seat is shown (§9.1) and the one effects count. Taking it out of the hand at step 1 instead reorders §10.5 for every reader at steps 2 and 3 (a Tribute's Death that draws would meet a hand one card smaller), a rules change far wider than this row, for a case no Core card reaches: no Core card has an `onPlayHook` (R153), and no Core Death discards | §10.1, §10.5, R90, R210 |
| R227 | A card set face-down takes a fresh id | A Trap or Field Trap that goes face-down into a backrow — played from hand, cast (R70) or recruited (§6.3) — takes a fresh instance id as it goes, the next number, as a new card takes one. So the only handle the action protocol has for a face-down card (a play's target, a prompt option's answer, R177) is an id no player has seen before: an id seen while the card was public — a trap returned from its owner's graveyard to hand and set again, a card revealed from a library — never names it in its zone. It is the same card, not a new one: its flags and its cost history go with it. The `cardPlayed` and `summoned` events that set it carry the id it had as `formerId`, so the events that named the old id are judged by where the card is now, exactly as they were before (R97), and its controller's client finds the hand card it came from; a view that hides the card hides `formerId` too. Whether a card goes face-down is public (§10.8 shows the zone occupied), so the number it takes says nothing. A Unit, a Field Spell, and a face-down card that moves or changes controller keep their ids | §3.2, §9.1, §10.5, R33, R97, R177, R223 |
| R240 | An absorbed fatigue draw is reported | A fatigue draw (§2.4, R3) is a draw that happened even when the hero's Armor takes its whole hit (R125; §4.4 step 3's cap only clamps a hit, to 5 or 3, and never takes all of it): the fatigue count both seats read goes up and the next one deals more. §4.4's zero rule leaves that hit no `damage` event, which would leave a visible change with none (§10.3), so the draw is reported by a `damage` event of 0 from no source on that hero. It is a report and not a damage instance (R63): no trap and no trigger answers it, and nothing else about the pipeline changes | §2.4, §4.4, §10.3, R3, R63, R125, #84, #90 |
| R241 | An end-of-turn clause belongs to the turn it was made on | §6.2's "End of turn" is the controller's turn end, and a delayed effect a card arms for it (#39's copies, #78's exile) is the end of the turn the card was played on. One armed on the other player's turn — the card was cast on draw there (R70) — has no end of its controller's turn to wait for, so it is not armed at all: waiting for the next one would run it at the end of a later turn the card was never played on, when its own "this turn" riders have long ended (§2.2) and #39 would read a turn log it was never in (R71). R155 keeps a return Spell cast then in the graveyard for the same reason. Setup is no player's turn either (it runs at turn 0, BUILD M1-T1, though the engine names p1 active there until p1 takes the first turn, §2.1 step 5), so a clause a card cast during setup makes — a mulligan's replacement draw cast it — is not armed, for either seat alike. A clause armed on its controller's own turn, even as that turn's delayed effects resolve, still waits for its point as R68 says | §2.2, §6.2, R68, R70, R71, R155, #39, #78 |
| R242 | A random pick over cards read by different players | A random "becomes Radiant" pick (R60) whose zones mix cards everyone reads — a unit, a face-up backrow card — with cards only their owner reads (their hand, their face-down trap: §9.1, R33) or nobody reads (a library, §3) is made in two draws, so its odds never read a face a player may not. First it draws how many of its picks each of those three groups takes: `count` different slots drawn uniformly among them all, where a public card is a slot only while it is not Radiant (its face is public) and a hidden card is a slot whatever its face (that face is what the other player may not read). Then each group picks its share among its own non-Radiant cards uniformly (R60), and cues the picks it could not make on its own Radiant cards (R177). So the chance that a public card is picked, and how many picks land among each player's unread cards, hang on the groups' sizes alone, which both players can count: drawn from the non-Radiant cards alone, #28's two picks always took a public unit when every hidden card was Radiant and sometimes passed it over when one was not, which told the other seat that a hidden card was base-face. The events go out group by group — the public cards', then the owner's hidden ones, then the library's — each in the zones' own order (hand order, lane order, the library top down), since in the zones' order a hidden pick's place beside a public one said whether it was a hand card, before the unit row, or a face-down trap, after it. When every slot is taken, or the zones hold one group only, nothing is drawn for the split and the pick is R60's as written (R129). Hearthstone has no face-down cards on the board, so this decides what it does not | #28, R60, R129, R177 |
| R243 | The view carries what a card is made of | §10.8 gives each viewer what they may read, and the client renders the view alone (§10.10, CLAUDE.md rule 7), so what a viewer may read about a card that its printed definition does not say is in the view, not only in an event that scrolls out (R169). A Unit card in its owner's hand shows its owner its stats as they stand — its face (the radiant one when it is Radiant, a fused card's summed one) plus the permanent buffs it has gained there (§10.4 layers 1, 3 and 4: #89 Corpse Eater's meals), attack floored at 0 — and a #98 Heroic Power in hand names the power it rolled as it arrived (R43, R151), which its cost does not (four of the seven powers cost 1). A unit whose text a Vanilla took (§6.3, R115) is marked Vanilla in both seats' views, since its definition still names the keywords and scripts it no longer has (#61's copy of #91). And a definition made during the match — a Fuse's (R77) or a crafted card's (R102, R179) — travels in the view beside it, by id, whenever the view names a card of it that the viewer may read; a card the viewer may not read is the sentinel by then (R97), so its definition stays in the match | #61, #85, #89, #98, #99, R77, R169, R179 |
| R244 | Who is dealt The Coin, and when | Hearthstone gives the player going second The Coin after the mulligan, and the source now says so too (JackiOh_Mechanics.md, at the user's request of 2026-09-24), without fixing the moment exactly. Player 2, who takes the second turn, is dealt one (§7) once both mulligans are answered and before §2.1 step 4's start-of-game effects, as the last card of their hand: never offered in, returned by or redrawn for a mulligan (R9). It is an add to hand, not a draw (§6.3): `addedToHand` alone, #100's draw counter unmoved (R55), and a full hand would burn it to the graveyard like any card (§2.4, R4), though no Core opening hand gets near one (five cards at most, R182). The seat going second gets it whatever its handicap: a handicap is a tier's resources (R180), and The Coin answers going second, so a Medium or Hard AI seated second holds five opening cards and The Coin, a human seated second against an AI gets it the same way, and the seat going first gets none at any tier. Setup deals it from the seed and the decks alone, with no rng draw, so a game still folds exactly from `(seed, decks, handicaps, log)` (§9.3, R187). It changes neither the turn order nor the cap: Player 1 still takes the first of the 60 player-turns (R389) and Player 2 the last (§2.5, R2). A Coin in hand is always an affordable play, so, like any 0-cost card, it keeps R82 from ending its holder's turn by itself | §2.1, §2.4, §2.5, §6.3, §9.9, R4, R9, R55, R82, R180, R182, R187, R389 |
| R245 | What The Coin is | The Coin (T-coin) is a Token-tagged Spell costing 0: "Gain 1 mana this turn", Radiant "Gain 2 mana this turn". The mana is §2.3's temporary mana: it adds to current mana, may take it past the cap (4, or a handicapped seat's `manaCap`, R181), and the next refresh takes it away, as with #6 Mana Well's. Every card has a Radiant face (§5.2), and a hand card can be made Radiant (#23, #26, #27, #29, #93's B step) or become Radiant as it is played (#64), so the Coin's doubles its number as #51.1's does (§8 Conventions). As a Token it is in no deck (§2.6, §9.4 L3, R184), an AI's included, and in no Discover, determinized hidden card or random pool but C+ #23 Dropshipping's, which takes every token (§5.1, R54, R185, R382): §2.1 deals one, C+ #42 KY's Test's Easy reward names three, and beyond those only Dropshipping or an effect that copies one (#33, #39, R34) makes one. Otherwise it is an ordinary spell token: playing it is a play for every rule that counts or answers plays (Combo, #38, #60, #64, #100, R70), it takes #79 Twinspell's Echo, and it goes to the graveyard when it resolves (R11), where #72 Reminisce can find it (R50) | §2.3, §5.1, §5.2, §7, R11, R34, R50, R54, R70, R181, R184, R185, R382, R420, C+ #23, C+ #42 |
| R247 | KY's Trial offers numbers | §8 #82 Discovers "among 3 distinct random numbers 1–100", and the prompt offers exactly that: each option is a Core card's §5 index, keyed and labelled by the number, and the answer adds the Radiant Core card with that index (R54, R74). Neither the option nor anything the view attaches to it names the card behind a number (§10.8), so the chooser is shown three numbers and not three cards, and a client draws them as numbers. Nothing is hidden by it: the catalog, which prints every card's index, is public (§5.1, §9.4), so a player may know which card a number is — knowing the Core set by number is the trial — and the options are simply what the card says they are. Every other Discover still offers the cards themselves, by name | #82, R54, R74 |
| R250 | Saved decks are drafts | A profile saves up to `MAX_SAVED_DECKS` (10) named decks. A save checks structure only — D1 a name of 1 to `DECK_NAME_MAX_LENGTH` (40) characters once trimmed, with no control characters; D2 at most `DECK_SIZE` cards; D3 every card a deckable card of the catalog (it exists and is not a Token); D4 at most `MAX_COPIES` of a card — and never legality: an incomplete deck, a card the player does not own and a banned card are all saved, and judged when the deck is queued (R253). A refused save names its first issue and lists at most `DRAFT_ISSUES_REPORTED_MAX` (50) of them. The loadout this replaces was one all-or-nothing save of three legal decks, so a half-built deck could not be kept at all, and the brief asks for the opposite: "save partial progress" and "never lose work" | §2.6, §9.4 |
| R251 | What "a card" is across a trio | A card is its catalog id. Radiant is a flag on a card in play (§5.2), never a second id, so a deck cannot hold a Radiant copy and "no card shared" compares catalog ids; Token-tagged cards are never deckable (§2.6). The builder's mark on a card "used in" another deck of the trio and L4 read the same fact, one list of shared ids | §5.2, §9.4 L4 |
| R252 | Trios | A profile saves up to `MAX_SAVED_TRIOS` (5) named trios, each three ordered slots naming the profile's own saved decks, any of them empty: T1 a name as D1, T2 three slots, T3 no deck twice. A trio is a draft like a deck: decks that share cards may be saved together, and the builder marks every shared card with the deck that also holds it. Deleting a deck empties every slot that named it and keeps the trio | §9.4 |
| R253 | What may be queued | A Best-of-1 deck must pass L2, L3, L5 and L6, the §9.4 rules about one deck, and a Conquest trio (R330) all of L1–L6, an empty slot failing L1: a trio is the loadout §9.4 first described. Checked by the shared validator at enqueue and at room create and join, with the decks' own names in its messages; the deck or trio is then frozen into the ticket or the room. The catalog version a client sends is checked at save; a saved deck's own version is no reason to refuse it, because the queue checks its cards against the current catalog (L6) | §9.4, §9.5, R165 |
| R254 | The loadouts that existed | Migration 0007 turns every saved loadout into three saved decks, named and filled as its three decks were, plus one trio named "My trio" holding them in their slot order. The loadout tables stay where they are, no longer read or written, so nothing a player saved is lost | §9.4 |
| R255 | Deck codes | A deck is shared as a code: `JKO`, `DECK_CODE_VERSION`, a dot, then base64url of the deck's name, its cards as catalog numbers and a checksum. Since patch v0.2.0 `DECK_CODE_VERSION` is 2 and each number carries its set, since a §5 index is unique only within one (R380): Core n, Classic 1000 + n, Classic+ 2000 + n, still LEB128; a version 1 code, as every code minted before it is, is read as Core numbers. Input longer than `DECK_CODE_MAX_INPUT_LENGTH` (512) is refused unread, as are a wrong prefix, a version other than 1 or 2 and a failed checksum, each with its own sentence. An import always makes a new deck: numbers the catalog does not have and Tokens are dropped, copies past `MAX_COPIES` and cards past `DECK_SIZE` are dropped, every drop is reported, and cards the player does not own are kept and marked, since a deck is a draft (R250). The code is the client's: the server checks the saved deck like any other | §5, §9.4, R380 |
| R256 | Autosave | Every deck and trio has an id the client mints, so saving one is an idempotent upsert of it alone that can be retried after a dropped connection without making a second. The builder saves `DECK_AUTOSAVE_DEBOUNCE_MS` (800) after the last edit, keeps every unsaved edit in the browser's storage, retries a failed save after `DECK_AUTOSAVE_RETRY_SECONDS` (5), and on return puts unsaved local edits back over the server's copy; it always shows which of Saved, Saving, Offline (kept on this device) or Couldn't save is true. A refused save is shown and not retried until the player edits again | §9.4 |
| R257 | Queue modes | The queue and the room challenge offer Best of 1 (one saved deck), Conquest (a trio, R330; called Best of 3 before it, and still `bo3` on the wire and in the database) and All Random (R258). A ticket pairs only with a ticket of the same mode; inside a mode §9.5's window and R166's order are unchanged, and the queue population is reported per mode. A request that names no mode is Best of 1 and may name its deck by its place in the saved list, oldest first (`deckIndex`), which is what a client from before the modes sends | §9.5, R166 |
| R258 | All Random | Both players are dealt a deck by the game's weighted random deck-builder — §9.9's curve- and tag-aware draw, with nothing banned, the deck practice deals a human who asks for a random one — seeded from the match seed and the seat, and the dealt decks are frozen into the match, so `(seed, decks, log)` still replays it. The collection is not consulted and no saved deck is needed | §9.5, §9.9 |
| R259 | The trio series (amended by R330–R336) | Until 2026-09-25 a series was Best of 3: first to two game wins, each deck played once, in at most three games, with a pick that could be changed until the other player picked. Conquest replaced that (R330–R334). What stands of this row: a series is played from the trio each player froze when they queued, one deck per side per game; a pick is hidden from the other side until both have picked, and the game starts as soon as they have (sealed now, R331); game n's seed is the series seed and n, and the seats alternate (R335); neither side is shown the other's deck names or lists (R336) | §9.5, R330, R331, R335, R336 |
| R260 | The pick clock (replaced by R333) | Picking closes `SERIES_PICK_SECONDS` (60) after it opens, and a series in which neither player has picked by then is abandoned, with no winner and no rating move. R333 keeps both, and gives a player who has not picked their first deck that has not won, where this row gave them their first deck not yet played | §9.5, R79, R333 |
| R261 | Endings inside a series (replaced by R334) | Each game ends as §2.5 says, a concede or a disconnect loses that game and not the series, a drawn game counts for neither side, and between games a player may forfeit the series, which the other side then wins: R334 keeps all of it. What R334 replaced is the end: after three games more wins took the series and a drawn game still spent both decks; now a draw locks nothing and the cap is `SERIES_MAX_GAMES` (7) | §2.5, §9.5, R334 |
| R262 | How a series is rated | A ranked series moves each rating once, when it ends: a win, a loss or a draw scored as one Glicko-2 game between its two sides (R603), from the ratings at the time it ends. Its games are recorded in `results` with both ratings unchanged. An abandoned or unranked series is unrated | §9.5, §9.12, R603, R604 |
| R263 | A series survives a restart | A series is a database row, written only by compare-and-set on its version, and a game's result, the series' record of it and, when the game ends the series, the rating move commit in one transaction. The next game's match id is reserved when its pick phase opens. A sweeper every `SERIES_SWEEP_INTERVAL_SECONDS` (5) runs the pick clock and starts any game whose picks are in and whose match is not running once the series has sat unchanged for `SERIES_START_GRACE_SECONDS` (15), so a restart between the picks and the match heals itself. A game that still has no match `SERIES_START_GIVE_UP_SECONDS` (120) after its picks cannot be started — its frozen decks no longer build a game, after a catalog change say — and the series ends abandoned, unrated, with that game off the record, so no player is held in a series that cannot go on. A series that ends before its first game releases the id it reserved | §9.3, §9.5 |
| R264 | Rooms carry a mode | A room is created in a mode, with the host's deck or trio frozen into it. A joiner plays the room's mode: a join in another mode is refused with the room's mode named, so the client can ask for the right deck or trio. Joining a Conquest room makes the series; joining an All Random room deals both decks. A profile in a series that is not over can neither queue nor create or join a room | §9.5, R257 |
| R265 | Both players mulligan at the same time | §2.1 step 3's two mulligans are open at once. Once the opening deal is done (a question it asked answered first, R224), both players' prompts open together, and either player may answer first. An answer changes nothing when it is given; the second one resolves both, always in seat order — Player 1's replacement draws and shuffle-back, then Player 2's (R9) — and then The Coin and the start-of-game effects follow (R244, R43). So the game dealt is the same whichever player answered first, `(seed, decks, log)` folds it in either order (§9.3), and it is the game the one-at-a-time mulligan dealt before. The two prompts are not `state.pending`, which stays §10.1's one prompt: they are one sealed-bid step (`state.mulligan`, §10.6), and while it is open nothing moves but a mulligan and the actions that end a game (R79, R211); a game that ends then closes both, as it closes an open prompt (R216). Hearthstone's mulligan is simultaneous too | §2.1, §9.9, §10.1, §10.2, §10.6, R9, R43, R216, R224, R244 |
| R266 | A mulligan answer is sealed | Until both players have answered, an answer is read by nothing: the answering player's hand stays as it was dealt, and the other player learns only that it is in (its `promptAnswered`, public like every prompt's, and the view's `mulligan.opponentReady`), never what was kept or offered (§9.1). Each player's view shows their own prompt until they answer, then `pending: { forYou: false, pendingFor: <the other player> }` and their own kept ids (§10.8); the AI's `redact` strips the other player's options and answer the same way (R185). Sealing is what makes the two simultaneous: an answer that resolved at once could show the other player a replacement's cast, a public play, before they had chosen | §2.1, §9.1, §9.9, §10.8, R97, R185 |
| R267 | A sealed answer meets the hand it resolves against | A player's answer is read when their turn to resolve comes (R265): the cards returned are the ones their prompt offered, that the answer did not keep, and that are still in that hand. Player 1's resolution can reach Player 2's hand before Player 2's resolves — a replacement draw's cast (§2.4, R70) that discards from it or adds to it — so a card that has left the hand is not returned, and a card that arrived after the prompt opened was never offered and stays. No Core cast-on-draw card touches the other player's hand; the rule is there so an answer never names a card its hand no longer holds | §2.1, §2.4, R9, R70, R224 |
| R268 | The mulligan's clock | The two mulligans share one clock: the server starts one deadline for both players when they open (`MULLIGAN_CLOCK_SECONDS`, 45 s, a server config value beside R79's), and one player's answer neither stops nor extends it for the other. When it runs out the actor sends a `timeout` for each player who still owes a mulligan, and a `timeout` while the mulligans are open answers only that player's own mulligan, by keeping the whole hand — Hearthstone confirms the hand as it stands when its mulligan timer runs out — with no draw from the rng. It never ends a turn there, since setup is no one's turn (§2.1). A match actor rebuilt while the mulligans are open (§9.5) starts their clock afresh, as it restarts the turn clock from full: the deadline is kept in the running actor, and the stored copy is written after the fact. R79's AI policy is not used for it: its uniform draw over the subsets would send back part of a hand the player never chose to send back | §2.1, §9.5, §10.2, R79 |
| R269 | How long a draw offer stands | An offer (R36) stands until the other player answers it or its offerer's turn ends. Nothing clears it at the turn's end, it simply stops standing, and one that lapses unanswered is not a declined one, so it blocks nothing (R36's block is a decline's). Both players' views carry the standing offer (`drawOffer: { by }`, §10.8): it was a public action (`drawOffered`), and a reconnect shows it (§9.5). How often a player may offer is R36's — once per turn, and not for three of their own turns after a decline — whose numbers are engine config values (`DRAW_OFFERS_PER_TURN`, `DRAW_OFFER_BLOCK_TURNS`), because hotseat and practice have no server to hold them. The answer comes only from the player who did not offer, and only while no prompt is open (§9.3). Practice's AI declines at once (R188); a hotseat hands the device to the other player to answer and back to the active player after, which is presentation and no rule | §2.5, §9.5, §9.9, §10.8, R36, R82, R188 |
| R270 | A client cannot send the server's nonce | The actor answers a nonce it has seen with the ack it gave then and applies nothing (§9.3), and it mints the nonces of its own actions — a clock's `timeout`, `disconnectExpired`, `ceilingReached` — from the log seq they will take, which a player can predict. So a client frame whose nonce starts with the server's prefix (`srv-`) is refused as malformed: otherwise a player could send the nonce of the next expiry first and swallow it, and a turn, or the other player's mulligan, would never time out short of the ceiling (R79, R268) | §9.3, §9.5, §9.8, R79, R268 |
| R275 | The Radiant power standard | §5.2 made Radiant an upgrade and the source says only "upgraded versions of normal cards", so how much stronger was never fixed, and a quarter of Core fell short of the rest (`docs/radiant-audit.md`). A Radiant face is about twice its base face. A Unit's attack and health are each at least double its base face's — a 0 stays 0, so #1 Big D-fender and #65.1 Spikey Pillow keep their 0 attack, and a token summoned X/X (the Bread Token, an Adaptive UI Ghoul Token) takes its X from the card that summons it, which scales X itself — and its effect is 100–150% stronger: 2 to 2.5 times the amount, a broader scope (one target to all, one side to a choice of sides), or an added rider or keyword, where a keyword-only unit's rider is one more keyword or a stronger one (Rush to Charge). A Spell, Field Spell, Trap or Field Trap scales its effect the same way and may add a draw or a tangential rider instead. A card whose base effect has no number is raised in scope or by a rider; an X-cost card's X is scaled (#24's 2X, #74's 2X and 3X); a formula is scaled as a formula (#31's Fib index moves two places, about 2.6 times; #40 and #70 double their exile term). The stat half is mechanical and a catalog-wide test holds every Unit face to it, listing any exception by name: there was none in Core, and patch v0.2.0 names two, both the designer's printed numbers — C #33 Joro's Radiant face stays 1/1 (a decoy whose Radiant Indestructible makes its health moot, R394) and C #80 BOOM! Big Max keeps its 26 attack (its health doubles and it gains Charge for Rush). An Animated card's unit face (R383) is held to it as a Unit is, and C+ #69 Buff Billy's X stats as multipliers. The effect half is the audit's judgement, recorded card by card, where the designer's word stands for C #22 Mid Runner, C #60 Pile On and C+ #3 Second Amendment Snake | §5.2, §7, §8, #8, #12, #24, #25, #31, #33, #38, #39, #40, #50, #54, #67, #68, #70, #73, #80, #81, #86, #87, #90.1, #93.1, #95.1, #96, #99, #100, T-rush, T-felinor |
| R276 | Every card has a Radiant face | §5.2 listed five cards with no Radiant form, which becoming Radiant left unchanged, so a Make Radiant spent on them (#26, #28, #29, #81, #93's B step) bought nothing, and R275 has nothing to hold them to. Each now has one: #38 Quickstriker's Combo deals 2X, #80 Zao Gao's Rush Tokens are Radiant, #93.1 Combo-Fodder deals 4, #95.1 Chaos Golem is a 20/20 with Charge for Rush, and #96 My Pawn also destroys the attacker. So every card's Radiant face differs from its base face, in its text, its stats or its keywords, and a test holds every catalog entry to that. R74's flag is unchanged: it is set, never unset, and now always changes the card | §5.2, §7, §8, R74, #38, #80, #93.1, #95.1, #96 |
| R277 | A Radiant face prints its whole text and marks what changed | The collection printed a Radiant face as its base clauses and then §8's shorthand cell ("9", "+3 damage") under a gold rule, which a player had to fit back into the sentence it changes. The catalog now carries each Radiant face's whole text — the cell read by §8's Conventions and written out — and a client prints it whole wherever a Radiant face's text is shown: the collection and its detail view, the hand, the field, a prompt's options, the showcase, a pile, the log's cards and every inspect overlay. The words and numbers in it that the base face's text does not have are marked: gold, and bold with an underline, so the mark does not rest on colour alone, in a gold dark enough to read on the light face and bright enough on the dark one. The mark is a word-level diff of the two catalog texts, compared without regard to case, so what it marks is public catalog text and the same in play as in the collection; a word the Radiant face drops is simply absent. A Radiant unit's attack and health, which R275 doubles, are marked the same way where a printed face shows them. It is presentation only: no rule reads it (CLAUDE.md rule 7) | §5.2, §8, §10.10, R243 |
| R278 | The Jlockeed tag | "Jlockeed" is a tag (§5) on the five cards whose names make them Jlockeed's: #13 Jlockeed Shredder-10, #14 Jlockeed's Weapons, and, since patch v0.2.0, C+ #48 Jlockheed's Lobbyist, C+ #51 Jlockheed's J15 Fighter and C+ #52 Jlockheed's Permanent Defense Contract, which keep the designer's spelling in their names and are one faction with Core's two under one tag (R381). It is no longer only a filter: C+ #48's and C+ #52's pools ("a random Jlockheed card") are the non-token cards with the tag, each leaving itself out (R387), so they find Core's two as well. No Core card's text or rule reads it, the deck builder offers it among its tag filters, and it is not a tribe any card counts. One spelling for the tag and every name would be a patch (R388) | §5, §5.1, §8, #13, #14, C+ #48, C+ #51, C+ #52; R381, R387, R388 |
| R279 | A card's text links the cards it names | A card's text names other cards and tokens — #90's CN-Virus, #41's Sheep Token and Lava Golem, #76's Reminisce — which a player could not read without leaving the card. The catalog lists them per card (`refs`, by id), and the list is proved against the texts both ways: every card or token whose name, or name before a parenthesis ("Call to Chaos", which #95 and C+ #73 share, so either edition's text names both), appears in a card's base or Radiant text, alone or plural, is in that card's `refs`, and every id in `refs` is named in one of its texts, a card naming itself included (#3, #65.1, #87, #95). Since patch v0.2.0 the proof keeps a named list of rules words — Exile, Burn, Echo, Recycle and the like — that it never reads as naming the card that bears the word (C #10 Exile, C #36 Burn, C #57 Echo, C #30 Recycle) unless that card's `refs` lists it, so a Core text's "Exile" or #79's "Echo 1" is no reference, and `refs` stay curated (R381). Every token is named by at least one card, or reached only through a pool a card's text names (C+ #12's Pancake tokens, the AI cards C+ #43 and C+ #78 hand out, and C+ #23's every-token pool, R382); The Coin, which a rule deals (§2.1, R244), is named by C+ #42 KY's Test's Easy reward. A client marks each such name as a reference, and where the surface lets a reference be a control (the collection's detail view, the touch inspect sheet) a mouse or pen resting on it, a click, the keyboard focusing it, or a tap on a touch screen shows that card's face beside it — its Radiant face where the text calls it Radiant ("a Radiant CN-Virus"), its base face otherwise — the reference is focusable and describes itself to assistive technology, and Escape closes the face and not the view around it. Where it cannot be a control (a face inside a button, a hover preview that takes no pointer events) the name is only marked, and the hover preview shows the faces of the other cards the text names beside it. The faces it shows are printed catalog faces, public by §5.1, so it reveals nothing; it is presentation, not a rule | §5, §7, §10.10, R244, #3, #15, #18, #41, #51, #58, #60, #62, #65, #65.1, #74, #75, #76, #80, #87, #90, #93, #95, #98, C #10, C #30, C #36, C #57, C #64, C+ #12, C+ #23, C+ #42, C+ #43, C+ #73, C+ #78; R381, R382 |
| R280 | The number a formula comes to now | A card whose text computes a number from the board by more than a plain X — a Fibonacci of the times it has been played (#31, R429), a sum over missing health and exile (#70), twice the exile (#40 radiant) — left the player to do the arithmetic, and a client may not (CLAUDE.md rule 7). The engine does it: a card's script may declare `preview`, a pure read (no writes, no random draws) that returns labelled numbers for the card's situation now — the label the formula as the running face prints it, the value what it would come to if this card alone resolved now — built on the same function its own resolution uses so the two cannot disagree. Something else resolving first can still move the number (two #40s at one start of turn: the first exiles a card before the second counts), which a value asked of one card cannot foresee. `viewFor` carries the result as `preview` on a card view the viewer may read and nowhere else: the viewer's own hand, a unit on top of its pile and a face-up backrow card (either seat's, since those are public), and a face-down card only for its controller; never an opponent's hand card, a library card or a card dormant under a Stack. A hook reads only what the card's controller may read (§9.1), and each Core hook says what: #18 the active player's current mana, #31 the times it has been played, #38 its controller's plays this turn, #40 and #70 its controller's exile count and hero health, #91 its own Plague Tokens — never a library's contents or order or a hidden hand. A client prints each value in braces after its label, "{7}", in hand and on the field; the collection prints none. Values the view already shows elsewhere are not repeated: #92's stats, #100's cost and #89's hand stats are on the face, and #24's and #74's X is chosen at play | §9.1, §10.8, §10.9, §10.10, R195, R429, #18, #31, #38, #40, #70, #91, C #1, C #19, C #43 |
| R281 | Radiant Quickstriker's damage is one hit | §8 #38's Radiant face grants "Combo X: deal 2X damage to the enemy hero", and 2X is dealt as one damage instance (§4.4), so Armor and Anti-oneshot Armor's cap apply to it once, as they do to the base face's X. Each Quickstriker on the field still grants its own Combo (R102, R119), each at its own face's multiple, so a base and a Radiant Quickstriker together deal X and then 2X. A card fused from Quickstrikers has one face (R77), so each Combo its texts grant is at that face's multiple: X each on its base face, 2X each on its Radiant one | #38, #73, #84, R77, R102, R119 |
| R282 | Radiant K-Pop Fanatic's rider rides its steal | §8 #50's Radiant face makes the stolen permanent Radiant, and it does so only to a card the delayed steal took: a target that has left the field (R76, R174), lies dormant under a Stack (R13), was already its controller's, or stays with the opponent because the row is full (R15) is not made Radiant, since the rider names "the stolen permanent" and there is none, and a Make Radiant on a card that has left the field could land in a hand the controller may not read. The rider is the face the Cry ran: the delayed steal carries it (§10.6, R126), so a base K-Pop Fanatic made Radiant after its Cry steals without the rider, and a Radiant one that has died since still applies it | #50, R13, R15, R76, R126, R174 |
| R283 | Radiant My Pawn destroys the attacker | §8 #96's Radiant face destroys the unit whose lethal attack it cancels, as part of the cancel and before the AI turn it hands over: an ordinary destroy (§6.3) of the attacker the cancelled declaration names, on the stay it declared from (R174), so an Indestructible attacker is knocked down instead (R46), a Reborn attacker comes back, and a My Pawn whose cancel finds no open attack — the second half of a My Pawn fused onto a My Pawn, which runs once the first has played the turn out (R102) — destroys nothing. The attack is cancelled either way (R44). The AI then takes over a settled board: inside the trap's list, between the destroy and the AI turn, the state check collects the attacker, the traps answer what it said, and the ordinary triggers its death woke resolve (a Corpse Eater in the AI's hand eats it) — as they would between two actions of a turn, which the AI's are — while the triggers the enclosing action had queued before keep their place (R117). This is a check inside one effect list, an exception to R59's check after the whole list made because the list's last effect is a whole turn; C+ #32.3 Blade Storm's rounds are the other (R59) | #96, R44, R46, R59, R102, R117, R174, R212, C+ #32.3 |
| R290 | The tutorial opponent's handicap | §9.9's tiers raise the AI seat's resources; the tutorial needs one that lowers them. `AI_TUTORIAL` is a handicap like the three tiers (R180) — deck size 12, no mana bonus, mana cap 3, no extra opening cards and no extra draws — plus one new, optional handicap field, `heroHealth`: the health the seat's hero starts the game with, in place of §2's 30. Absent means 30, and `createGame` stores it on the seat only when it moves the hero off 30, so no tier's state, hash or replay changes. A handicap's `heroHealth` is a positive integer; the tutorial's is 20. Nothing else about the hero changes: its health has no upper cap (§3), fatigue and every damage rule apply, and an effect that sets health to a number (#53 Reno's 30) does exactly that. The tutorial handicap is not one of `DIFFICULTIES`: no player picks it, the tutorial deals it. The AI's search, evaluation and budget stay every tier's (§9.9), so the opponent is easier by its resources alone, and the lesson decks (R291) do the rest | §2, §2.3, §2.6, §9.9, §9.10, R180, R181, R184 |
| R291 | The tutorial's lessons | A lesson is a practice game (R187) with a fixed seed, a fixed seat for the human and two fixed decks, named by the lesson's id in the worker's start; nothing else the page sends changes them. The human's deck is a legal §2.6 deck of 20, mostly Common, Rare and Epic cards (at most two Legendary or Mythic). The AI's is a fixed list of exactly `AI_TUTORIAL.deckSize` distinct non-token cards in place of `buildAiDeck`'s draw: none on the shadow ban (R186) unless the lesson names it with its reason — the ban keeps a card out of a dealt deck because a sweep saw the AI hold it unplayed at some tier, and a fixed list's own tests show what the AI does with it (a lesson has named one: #3, whose Taunt, Divine Shield and Reborn lesson 2 teaches, until patch v0.1.1's sweep took it off the ban; no lesson's list holds a banned card since) — none Legendary or Mythic, none that reads its hero's health against 30 (#53 Reno would lift a hero that starts at 20 to 30, and #70 Spiteful Stab would count ten health as already missing, R72), and none the lessons have not taught yet — units only, none whose keywords change combat, in lesson 1, and no Trap, Field Trap or Field Spell before lesson 3. The Coin goes to the seat going second as always (R244), so the lesson that teaches The Coin seats the human second and every other lesson seats the human first | §2.1, §2.6, §9.9, §9.10, R72, R184, R186, R187, R244, #3, #53, #70 |
| R292 | What the coach reads, and how it moves on | The coach reads what the page already holds — `viewFor(state, human)`, the human's `legalActions` and whether the AI owes an action — and nothing else, so it can show no more than the board does, and it decides nothing (CLAUDE.md rule 7). A step that asks for an action points at it and waits: the engine accepts or refuses whatever the player does, and the coach offers an attack or a play only while `legalActions` holds it. A step shows once its moment comes, completes when the view shows it done (even when the player did it before the step showed), and retires without a word when it can no longer happen. A step still current after `TUTORIAL_STEP_TURNS_MAX` (2) of the player's own turn starts retires by itself, except the last, which ends with the game; and the coach is finished once the game is over. There is no Skip step (R314). A tip shows once, the first time its moment comes. A tip or an `info` step that asks the player to read something first may hold the AI's next step until it goes, as §9.9's pacing holds for a voice line; an `act` step never does (R314) | §9.9, §9.10, §10.8, R187 |
| R293 | Every lesson is won by following the coach | For every lesson: a player who does what the coach asks, and the simplest sensible move where it asks nothing, wins; every step shows and completes on that line; and each mechanic the lesson lists actually comes up on it. A sensible player who ignores the coach also wins, and a player who ignores it with random legal moves neither breaks it nor stalls it. Each is a test that plays the lesson through the real practice core with the gates' budget and a frozen clock, where the AI's decisions are a pure function of the game (R187) | §9.10, R291, R292 |
| R294 | Tutorial progress is kept on the device | Which lessons a player has won is kept in the browser (`localStorage`, read and written inside try/catch); an active account keeps a copy on the server too (R320), merged with the device's (R321), and without one nothing is sent: the tutorial needs no account (§9.9) and none of it is a rule. Lesson 1 is always open, lesson n opens once lesson n − 1 has been won, and a won lesson stays won. A lesson counts as won the moment the view shows the human's victory. Storage that is blocked or fails to write keeps progress in memory for the session | §9.9, §9.10 |
| R310 | A player's own library, without its order | §9.1 hides a library's order from both players and its contents from the opponent; nothing requires hiding the contents from their owner, who built the deck, drew and returned their own opening hand, and watched every card go in that went in openly. So `viewFor` gives the viewer their own library as a list without order (`SideView.ownLibrary`): one entry per definition and face with a count, sorted by the definition's printed cost (R65: X is 0, an embiggen card its base price), then its name, then its id, the base face before the Radiant one. That order depends on the cards alone, so two libraries holding the same cards in any order give the same view, and the list carries no instance id, no position, no cost as it stands (#95's discount, R177) and no rolled power (#98, R43). The opponent's library stays a count. A deck the player was dealt rather than built (All Random, R258; practice's fresh random deck) lists, since patch v0.2.0, only what its owner has been shown of it (R433): its starting cards are unknown until they leave the library, and what goes in openly afterwards is listed as for any deck | §3, §9.1, §10.8, R433 |
| R311 | What the owner was shown going in | Each library card records what its owner was shown of it as it went in, its definition and its face (`knownAs`, §10.1), and the list reads that record, never the card. The record is written where a card goes in openly: the starting deck, a mulligan's returns, and a shuffle-in, which in Core is always done by a card both players watched resolve: CN-Virus's copies, Unstable Clone Machine's copies, and the opponent's CN-Viral Injection, whose play is public and whose text names the virus and, on its Radiant face, the virus's face. A change made inside a library, where nobody reads it, does not show: a card #28 picks or #42 rolls Radiant there is listed with the face it went in with until it leaves, and #95's discount and #98's roll never show | §10.1, §10.8, #28, #33, #42, #90, #90.1 |
| R312 | Cards the owner was never shown | A library card with no record counts as unknown, and a client draws it as a card back.
