That is every card of a library Pocket Chaos swapped (#87, R73): its new owner never saw it, and what its old owner was shown goes with neither, so a library swapped back is unknown too. It is also every card Transmogulate puts in a library (#83, R35), a new instance that the `transformed` event names to nobody (R177). A card stays unknown until it leaves the library, even one a prompt has since shown its owner (#51's options): the list may show less than its owner could piece together, never more. A path that forgets to write the record shows a back, never a card. The practice AI hides the same cards from its own seat when it redacts the state it decides from (R185) | §9.1, §9.9, §10.8, #51, #83, #87 |
| R313 | Looking through your library | The client draws the list the view gives and nothing else (CLAUDE.md rule 7). On the viewer's own library pile a resting mouse opens a preview, and a click, a tap, a long-press or Enter opens a dialog, as for a graveyard (§10.10). The title gives the size and says the order is hidden ("Your deck — 14 cards (order hidden)", in players' words since R373). The faces are grouped with their counts in the view's order, each printed on the face it went in with and with no cost from play, and unknown cards are backs. The opponent's library pile opens nothing. It is the same board in hotseat (for the seat whose view is shown), online, practice and the tutorial | §10.10 |
| R314 | The tutorial has no Skip step | The user asked on 2026-09-25 for the tutorial's Skip step to go, from the HUD and from the coach's bubble. Nothing needs it to keep a player moving. Only a tip or an `info` step may hold the AI, and each shows Got it; an `act` step never holds it, whatever its script says. A step still current after `TUTORIAL_STEP_TURNS_MAX` of the player's own turn starts retires by itself, except the last, which ends with the game. A step never blocks an action, since the engine takes whatever the board sends, and Exit tutorial is in the HUD throughout | §9.10, R292 |
| R315 | Fatigue is reported before its hit | §2.4 and R3 made the Nth draw from an empty library deal N damage, and the only event it produced was that damage instance: a hit from no source that looked like any other, and none at all to a viewer whose hero's Armor took it whole (R240's zero report aside). Every fatigue draw now emits `fatigue { player, count, amount }` first — `count` is the owner's fatigue count after the draw (the Nth), `amount` the hit before Armor, `FATIGUE_DAMAGE(count)` — and its `damage` follows at once, R240's zero report when Armor takes it all. It names no card, and the fatigue count is already public (§10.8), so both players read it in full. It is a report, like R240's: no card answers it. A draw #75 Infinite Reserves replaces is not a fatigue draw and emits none; the Rush Token card's `drawn` reports it. The draw itself is unchanged: the same count, the same hit, the same rng | #75, §2.4, §10.3, R3, R240 |
| R316 | A full library reports what it turned away | R80 had a card that would be shuffled into a full library either never created (#33's and #90's copies) or sent to its owner's graveyard (an existing card; a unit-token card ceases to exist, R11), and the first case emitted nothing at all. Every refusal now emits `libraryOverflow { player, instanceId, defId, outcome }`, with `player` the library's owner and `outcome` `notCreated`, `graveyard` (followed by the card's `enteredGraveyard`) or `ceased`, and `radiant` when the refused card is Radiant (#33's Radiant face), which a view hides with the card. No Core card shuffles an existing card into a library, so a Core card's refusal is always `notCreated`; C #30 Recycle does, and a card of it that does not fit stays in its owner's graveyard (R80), while `ceased` is R80's rule made whole. The card is judged by R97: one sent to the graveyard reads while it is there. One never created is in no pile, so it is judged by the card it would have copied: #33 copies whatever its controller plays, a Trap set face-down included, and a refused copy of that trap names it to the other player no more than the trap does, until the trap is public. The event carries the copied card's id for this (`copyOf`), which the view never forwards. A card that copies nothing (#90's CN-Virus) reads openly to both, as the play that made it did. A refused existing card has moved, as a `shuffledIn` card has (R212); a copy never created has not. The copy still takes its instance id from the counter as it did before, so no later id moves | #33, #90, #90.1, §2.4, §10.3, R11, R80, R97, R212, C #30 |
| R317 | What each player sees of a burned card | §2.4's full hand sends a drawn or added card to the graveyard, and `burned` is the event that says so: it is the hand cap's only event, so it is what "hand full" means on the board (R318). A burned card never enters the hand, and R97 judges it where it is now, the owner's graveyard (or nowhere, a unit-token card having ceased to exist, R11), so both players read which card burned, and read the `drawn` that brought it too; Hearthstone shows both players a burned card the same way. If the card later goes somewhere a player may not read it (#72 Reminisce returns it to its owner's hand), that player's copy of the event is the sentinel from then on, and the board shows a back | #5, #17, #72, #94, #95, §2.4, R4, R11, R97 |
| R318 | The board shows the three overflows | The client shows each of §2.4's overflows on both seats from its event alone (CLAUDE.md rule 7). `fatigue`: the owner's library pile shakes and dims as it comes up empty and a "Fatigue N" badge (the event's `count`) rises from it, then the `damage` after it lands on the hero with its number; a hit Armor took whole still shows the badge, and the hero takes R240's hit of 0. `burned`: the card rises over its owner's hand under a "Hand full" tag, catches fire and burns away toward the graveyard, showing its face when the viewer reads the event and a back when the event carries the sentinel. `libraryOverflow`: a "Deck full" tag (R373) flashes on the owner's library pile and the refused card, face or back by the same test, bounces off it and fizzles into smoke (`notCreated`, `ceased`) or drops toward the graveyard (`graveyard`). The notices are the board's own elements, named by testid, and stay up until the board shows the next view, as a damage number does, each entry drawing its own, so #33's three refused copies are three refusals; a fatigue hit that ends the game, which the finished view's drain cuts short, is replayed by the effects layer with the empty library it came out of (R200's killing blow); the motion is BUILD M5-T4's rows, whose durations R201's speed scales and reduced motion zeroes, when nothing is drawn at all. The effects layer adds its recipes (`fatigue`, `overflow`, `burn`) under R200 and R202. Like every event, each plays once on each seat, however R97 has rewritten the window it arrives in (the event diff of §10.10) | §2.4, §10.10, BUILD M5-T4, R97, R200, R201, R202, R315, R316, R317 |
| R319 | The overflows' sounds | Each of the three has a sound of its own, played when the runner starts its entry (§10.11): `fatigue` a hollow knock on the empty library before the hit's impact, `libraryOverflow` a dull two-note refusal, `burned` the burn crackle. None varies with the card, so a card behind the sentinel sounds like any other (R203), and none speaks a line, being no play, cast, death or trap firing (R204) | §10.11, R203, R204, R315, R316, R317 |
| R320 | Tutorial progress kept on the account | An active account keeps its tutorial progress on the server as well as on the device (R294), so a player who signs in on another device finds it there: one `tutorial_progress` row per profile, holding the ids of the lessons it has won and its newest Hide/Show choice (R322) with the time that choice was made. A player reads only its own row and writes none of it directly (RLS, §9.1). The server writes it through `GET` and `PUT /api/tutorial`, which are `active` routes (a pending account has only the code screen, §9.4) and take the profile from the verified token, never from the body. A write merges and never replaces: the stored lessons become the union of the stored and the sent, so a stale device can never remove a completed lesson; and the stored choice is replaced only by one made strictly later, a time after the server's clock counting as now, so a device whose clock runs ahead cannot pin its choice. The body is checked first: at most `TUTORIAL_LESSONS_MAX` (32) distinct lesson ids, each a lower-case slug of at most `TUTORIAL_LESSON_ID_MAX_LENGTH` (40) characters, and a choice that is a boolean with a whole-millisecond time. The server does not know the lessons, which are the client's: an id it is sent is kept, and a client that does not list it ignores it. None of it is a rule: a lesson is still a practice game and records no result (R187), and nothing the server does reads the row | §9.1, §9.4, §9.9, §9.10, R187, R294 |
| R321 | Merging the device's progress with the account's | The device's progress and the account's are merged as a union of completed lessons, and for Hide/Show the newest explicit choice wins, by the time it was made on the device that made it (a new choice is always stamped later than the one it replaces, and a tie keeps the device's). So a completed lesson never becomes uncompleted, and Show on one device is not undone by an older Hide on the account. On `/practice`, a page with an active account reads the account's progress once, merges it into the device, and sends the merged progress up if the device holds something the account lacks: a lesson it does not list, or a later choice that says otherwise. After that, each change on the device (a lesson won, Hide or Show) is sent up as it happens, one request at a time, and the answer merged back in. The device's copy is the one the page renders and is authoritative for the session: a request that fails or never answers changes nothing on screen and raises nothing, and the next load catches the account up. Signed out, pending or banned, progress stays on the device and nothing is sent (R294). Progress belongs to the device as much as to the account, so signing in on a device adds that device's lessons to the account | §9.9, §9.10, R294, R320, R322 |
| R322 | Hiding the lesson path | While a lesson is still to do, the lesson path's header offers Hide tutorial. It folds the whole path to one line in its place ("Tutorial hidden", the lessons done, and Show tutorial, which brings the path back). Both are buttons that take keyboard focus, focus moves to the one that undoes the press, and both are 44 px touch targets that fit a phone's width. The choice is stored with the progress, on the device and on the account (R321), and holds whatever the progress: a path hidden before its last lesson stays hidden after it. A finished path offers no Hide, because it already folds to its header by itself (§9.10), and its Show lessons opens it for the session as before | §9.10, R294, R321 |
| R323 | Emailed links carry a PKCE code, not tokens | The implicit flow put a session's tokens in the link's URL fragment, where history, a shared screen or a referrer could read them. So the three mailers (the sign-up's confirmation, its resend and the password reset) send a `code_challenge`, the SHA-256 of a random verifier in base64url, with `code_challenge_method` `s256`, and keep the verifier in the browser's `localStorage` inside try/catch, one per kind of link; a resend reuses the sign-up's verifier, so the first email's link keeps working. The link comes back to `/login?code=…`, or to the Site URL `/`, which moves it to `/login`. The code is read on those two paths only, is scrubbed from the address bar before anything renders, and is exchanged at `/auth/v1/token?grant_type=pkce` with `auth_code` and `code_verifier`, newest verifier first; one the provider answers `bad_code_verifier` belongs to another link and the next is tried. The session that comes back is judged exactly as R193 judges a link's session: a confirmation signs nothing in and is revoked, and a recovery is held only for the reset this browser asked for (or once the player types its address). No token is ever in a URL, so nothing in history needs spending | §9.2, R193, R194 |
| R324 | A link opened elsewhere, and a link from before | A code this browser holds no verifier for was opened on another device or browser, or after its storage was cleared. The provider sends a code only after it has confirmed the address, so `/login` says "Your email is confirmed. Sign in to continue." rather than showing an error; a reset link opened there cannot be used, and the forgot-password form asks for a new one. A code the provider refuses (spent or expired) reads as R193's spent link, with its ways forward. A link mailed before the switch, or mailed to a browser that could not hash (no `crypto.subtle` outside a secure context, where the mailer sends no challenge), carries the implicit flow's tokens in its fragment and is read exactly as before. The Supabase project's Site URL (`https://jackioh.vercel.app`) and Redirect URLs (`https://jackioh.vercel.app/**`, `http://localhost:5173/**`) are dashboard settings, listed in docs/architecture.md's bring-up checklist | §9.2, R193 |
| R330 | Conquest | The trio mode is Conquest (its queue name stays `bo3`, R257): a player takes the series by winning one game with each deck of their trio, `SERIES_WINS_NEEDED` (3) game wins. A deck that wins a game is locked for the rest of the series, so no deck wins twice and a side's wins are its decks that have won; a deck that lost or drew may be picked again, as often as its player likes. Hearthstone's Conquest format is the model. Which decks have won is read off the series' record of its games, so the series row R263 describes kept its shape and needed no migration. It replaces R259's first to two wins with each deck played once | §9.5, R257, R259, R263 |
| R331 | The sealed pick | Before each game both players pick at once, from their own decks that have not won (R330), the way the concurrent mulligan is answered (R265, R266): a pick is final once made — a second pick for the same game is refused, and the same pick sent again is answered as the success it was, even after the clock ran out, so a retried request is harmless; a pick names the game it is for, and one naming another game is never applied, so a late duplicate of one game's pick cannot become the next game's — and until both are in, the other player learns only that it is in ("Opponent has picked"), never which deck. The pick that completes both starts the game at once. A pick is written to the series row before it is acknowledged, so a server restart keeps it (R263), and it leaves the server only in its owner's projection (§9.1). It replaces R259's pick, which could be changed until the other player picked | §9.1, §9.5, §9.8, R259, R263, R265, R266 |
| R332 | The last deck is picked for you | When a pick phase opens and a player has one deck left that has not won, it is picked for them; when both players are down to one, there is nothing to choose and the game starts at once, without a pick phase. A pick made this way is a pick like any other: sealed, shown to the other player only as "picked", and one for R333's clock. It widens R259's "the third game's picks are made for them" | §9.5, R259, R333 |
| R333 | The pick clock | Picking closes `SERIES_PICK_SECONDS` (60) after it opens: one deadline for both players, which a pick neither stops nor extends. A player who has not picked by then is given their first deck in trio order that has not won, and the game starts; if neither player has a pick in by then — a pick R332 made counts — the series is abandoned, with no winner and no rating move (R262). It replaces R260, which gave a late player their first deck not yet played | §9.5, R79, R260, R262, R332 |
| R334 | Draws, the game cap and leaving | A drawn game counts for neither side and locks neither deck (R330). Without a draw a series is decided by its fifth game at the latest (2 × `SERIES_WINS_NEEDED` − 1), and a series plays at most `SERIES_MAX_GAMES` (7) games, which leaves room for two drawn games in the longest series and stops a run of draws from holding both players for ever: when the cap is reached with neither side at `SERIES_WINS_NEEDED`, more wins takes the series and equal wins is a series draw (`exhausted`). Each game ends as §2.5 says: a concede or a disconnect loses that game, not the series. Between games a player may forfeit the series, which the other side then wins; during a game the way out is to concede the game. It replaces R261, whose cap was three games | §2.5, §9.5, R261 |
| R335 | Who goes first | Series seat p1 is the player who queued first, or hosted the room; it goes first in odd games and series p2 in even games. Drawn games count, so the seats alternate game by game whatever the results, and game n's seed is the series seed and n. This is R259's order, kept: alternating by game number is simple, known before anyone picks, and does not make the first seat a prize for losing | §9.5, R259 |
| R336 | What each side sees | The series screen and the board's series banner show the score and which of each side's decks have won and are locked: your own decks by name, with their cards and how many games each has played; the other side's only by slot number and whether each has won, and whether their pick is in. A game's history shows the slot each side played once the game has begun. Neither side is shown the other's deck names, card lists or pending pick at any point (§9.1) | §9.1, §9.8, R259, R330, R331 |
| R337 | A series under way when Conquest shipped | A series begun as R259's Best of 3 goes on as a Conquest series from its next change: every game it has played counts as Conquest counts it — a win locks the deck it was won with — so a series at one win each after two games does not end after its third, and one that R259 had already ended stays ended. Nothing in the row had to change for this (R330) | §9.5, R259, R330 |
| R338 | Where the pick happens | The pick is on the series screen, between games, not on the board: no match exists until both decks are known, because a match starts with both decks frozen into it (§9.4) and replays from `(seed, decks, log)` (§9.3), so the engine never learns there was a choice and a pick survives a restart in the series row. The screen offers it as the board offers the mulligan: the player selects a deck that has not won, locks it in, and waits ("Waiting for your opponent…") with the other player's readiness and the one clock on screen; a deck that has won is shown locked and cannot be selected, and a player on the screen when both picks are in is taken to the game. It is presentation: the server decides what may be picked (CLAUDE.md rule 7) | §9.3, §9.4, §9.5, R265, R266, R331 |
| R339 | Trio codes | A trio is shared as a code: `JKT`, `TRIO_CODE_VERSION`, a dot, then base64url of the trio's name, one byte saying which of its three slots hold a deck, each of those decks as R255 writes one (its name, then its cards as catalog numbers, each carrying its set), and one checksum over all of it. Since patch v0.2.0 `TRIO_CODE_VERSION` is 2, its decks written as R255's version 2 writes them, and a version 1 code is read as Core numbers. Input longer than `TRIO_CODE_MAX_INPUT_LENGTH` (2048) is refused unread, as are a wrong prefix, a version other than 1 or 2, a slot byte naming a fourth slot, a payload that declares more than it holds or holds more than it declares, and a failed checksum, each with its own sentence; a deck code pasted as a trio code, or a trio code as a deck code, is sent to the other import. Each deck is read as R255 reads one — what no deck could hold dropped and listed, unowned cards kept and marked — and a name D1 or T1 would refuse arrives as "Imported deck" or "Imported trio". Decoding never throws. The code is the client's, as a deck code is; the server checks what an import sends (R341) | §9.4, R251, R252, R255, R380 |
| R340 | A trio import fits under both caps or makes nothing | Importing a trio code makes one new deck per filled slot and one new trio naming them in their slots; nothing the player has is changed. If that would pass `MAX_SAVED_DECKS` or `MAX_SAVED_TRIOS`, nothing is made, and one sentence from the shared validator says how many deck and trio slots the import needs, how many are free and how many to delete; the workshop says it before the player asks, and the server says it again, with the numbers in `details`. Unowned cards and cards the imported decks share are kept, because a trio is a draft (R252): the preview says so, and the trio editor marks each shared card (R251) | §9.4, R250, R251, R252 |
| R341 | The server checks a trio import, all or nothing | `POST /api/trios/import` takes the decks and the trio the client decoded, with ids the client minted (R256), and checks them as any save is checked: the catalog version, D1–D4 for each deck (a refusal names the deck), T1–T3 for the trio, the body's shape and every id a UUID. It then checks R340's room and writes every deck, one millisecond apart in slot order so the saved list shows them in their slots' order, and then the trio, in one transaction, so a failure part-way leaves nothing behind; ids the caller already owns are a retried import and take no new slot, so the client may send the same import again after a lost answer, and does, with the same ids. It never goes through the autosave, which would leave half an import behind a refusal, and the workshop sends it only once every earlier edit has landed, so the caps it checked are the ones the server holds | §9.1, §9.4, R250, R252, R256, R340 |
| R345 | A player may turn the automatic turn end off | Each player's own preference, on by default, as R82 has it. `setAutoEndTurn {enabled}` sets it for its sender; it is accepted from either seat at any moment of a live game, the mulligans and an open prompt included; it emits nothing; and `legalActions` never offers it, so no policy ever sends it (§10.7). Off, a turn with nothing left in it but ending it, conceding and offering a draw waits for that player's End turn, and they may offer a draw on it (R36); the turn clock still runs (R79). Turned back on with nothing left to do, the turn ends at once, as R82 says. It is part of the state, so a match folds exactly from its log (§9.3), and it is stored only while off, so a game in which nobody touched it hashes as before. The view carries the viewer's own preference only (§10.8), never the opponent's. The web client sends it from its "End turn automatically" setting whenever the view disagrees, and a tutorial lesson keeps it on, since its coach is written around R82 | §2.5, §9.3, §10.2, §10.7, §10.8, R36, R79, R82 |
| R346 | Pierce | A new keyword (§6.1): a hit whose source has Pierce skips §4.4 step 2, on a unit and on a hero alike, and nothing else — Divine Shield, the hero cap and Indestructible still apply. A unit's Pierce is read through the layers like its Lifesteal and Trample, so a granted one counts; a spell's is printed on its face and read off the card while it resolves (#44 True Strike), and #44's effect states it as well, as R85's `lifesteal` states Lifesteal, so an Echo repeat that has no source left still pierces. My Pawn's lethal projection (R44) and Zephyrs' scorer read it the same way. Pierce joins R21's random-keyword pool, at the end | §4.4, §6.1, R21, R44, R85, #44, #63, #74, #80, T-ghoul |
| R347 | An Indestructible unit has no Taunt | A unit with Indestructible never has Taunt, whether printed, granted, from an aura or from Defense Position: §10.4's keyword set drops Taunt whenever it holds Indestructible, so an Indestructible unit in Defense Position has the Armor +1 and no Taunt, and §4.2 step 3 never makes it the only legal target. It is a reading, not a removal: take the Indestructible away (a Vanilla) and any Taunt the unit has is back, unless R46's stamp still names the turn. The patch drops Indestructible from the two faces that printed both, #55 and #56 radiant | §4.1, §4.2, §6.1, §10.4, R46, #55, #56 |
| R348 | X is at least 1 | An X-cost card whose X the player chooses — #24 Efficiency Dividend and #74 Adaptive UI; #98's X is its power's (R43) and no other Core card costs X — is played for 1 ≤ X ≤ current mana (`MIN_CHOSEN_X`). One check (`playChoices.whyXRefused`) is both what `legalActions` offers and what the play refuses, so a play naming X = 0, or naming no X, is refused ("X must be at least 1"), and with no mana the card cannot be played at all, even when a `costOverride` makes it free (R65) | §2.3, R65, R81, R90, #24, #74 |
| R349 | The Radiant fallback | A Unit that prints no Radiant form of its own is, made Radiant, its base face with its attack and health doubled (`RADIANT_FALLBACK_FACTOR`): the same keywords and text, and a summon's X/X (`statsOverride`) doubled with it, so a Radiant Ghoul Token summoned 3/3 is a 6/6. It is §10.4 layer 1's reading, so it holds whenever the flag is set, on the field at once like any Radiant swap (§5.2) with buffs and damage kept, and the X on the instance is never rewritten. The catalog marks such a card `radiantFallback` and prints the fallback as its Radiant face, which `validate-catalog` holds it to; in Core only the Ghoul Token is one. A fused or crafted card is not: R77 builds its Radiant form from its ingredients' Radiant forms, and an ingredient with none lends its fallback, its doubled X included | §5.2, §7, §10.4, R74, R77, R275, R276, T-ghoul |
| R350 | CN-Virus's copies wait for the end of the turn | #90.1's "shuffle copies of this into your deck" happens at the end of the turn it is cast on, whoever's turn that is — a draw can come on either player's turn (C #38 Jackiestan Auctioneer draws on every card either player plays) — as an end-of-turn delayed effect (§2.2, R62), in R68's creation order among that turn's others; its damage is still dealt at once. The copies go into the caster's own library, its owner's, with the flag of the face that was cast (R57), each at a random position, and R80's cap and R316's `libraryOverflow` apply to each. So a chain casts only the viruses the library held when it began (R58), and each cast arms its own shuffle. A virus cast after that turn's end-of-turn delayed effects have begun is due at the next such point for the same player (R62); one cast during setup, which is no player's turn, waits for the end of turn 1. R241 does not drop it, since "this turn" is never another player's | §2.2, §2.4, §7, R57, R58, R62, R68, R80, R241, R316, R426, #90, #90.1, C #38 |
| R351 | A face-down Trap shows its cost | A face-down Trap or Field Trap shows its cost to both players: the other player's view of its zone is `{ faceDown: true, cost }`, the cost the controller's own view shows (§6.3's cost read on the field: its `costOverride` or printed cost, plus its `costMod`, R65), and nothing more. It is a deliberate reveal, which the designer asked for; R33 is otherwise unchanged. The controller's own view marks each card the other player sees face down `unrevealed`, until it is public (a Field Trap that has fired). This row is the view's half; R370 draws the cost and R371 the mark, on the client | §3, §10.8, R33, R65, R97, R370, R371 |
| R352 | Stitching | #98 Heroic Power's eighth power, `stitching`, X 2: "Discover 2 Units that cost (2) or less. Fuse them and add the result to your hand." Two chained Discovers from the non-token Units whose out-of-play cost (R65) is 2 or less, each answer carried in the next prompt's data; the second answer fuses the two per R77 with no target on the field, and the result goes to the activating player's hand at R77's fused cost, min(sum, 4), since the power names no price (#99's "costs (0)" is #99's). The Radiant face Discovers Radiant Units and makes the result Radiant. The use is spent as the power is activated (R103); a Discover with no pool fizzles, and fewer than two picks fuse nothing. The start-of-game roll picks from the eight | §8 #98, R43, R77, R103, #98 |
| R353 | The Ghoul Token | A card of its own in patch v0.1.1: `core-t-ghoul`, index T-ghoul, a 0-cost Unit Token, printed 0/0 and always summoned X/X through `statsOverride` like the Bread Token, with Pierce (R346). The designer printed no Radiant form, so R349's fallback is its Radiant face. #74 Adaptive UI summons it where it summoned a Rush Token, X/X and on the radiant card 3X/3X, always on the token's base face | §5, §7, R11, R346, R349, #74 |
| R354 | Zao Gao, patched | #80's discard is random: two picks from the match rng over the hand as it stands, never a prompt (R16), fewer than two in hand discarding what there is. The Radiant face's tokens stay Radiant and each rolls three keywords where it rolled two, the designer's "Modify" read as a change to the face the Radiant pass gave it (R276) rather than a replacement of it. It carries the CN tag | §6.3, §7, §8 #80, §10.6, R16, R21, R221, R276, #80 |
| R360 | Lava Golem is summoned for the opponent | §8 #55's base face: "Can use opposing Units as Tributes. If opposing Units are used, summon for your opponent." A play whose Tribute takes at least one unit the opponent controls — read as §10.5 step 2 pays it, before those units die — puts the Golem, at step 4, into the opponent's zone in the lane the play named when that zone is open, else their leftmost open unit zone (R15's placement for a card that changes sides), under the opponent's control. It is still the player's play, so the `cardPlayed` is theirs and the opponent's traps answer it as a play by the opponent, and still the player's card, which goes to its owner's piles when it leaves the field (§3.2); it has entered its controller's side on that turn, as R171 has any change of side. With no open zone on the opponent's side once the Tribute is paid — a tributed Reborn unit's zone is reserved for its return (R64) — it lands where the play named, on the player's side, as a steal that finds no free zone leaves the card where it is (R15). The Radiant face keeps the permission and drops the clause, so it stays with the player whoever paid | #55, R15, R64, R101, R171 |
| R361 | "Miss" Mrow takes the unit that destroyed her | §8 #86's Death: "Take control of the Unit that destroyed this." The unit that destroyed her is R42's killer: the unit whose damage instance took her to 0 or less health, or whose Poisonous hit marked her — in combat, as attacker or defender, or through its own text's damage (#68's Cry). Her Death reads it off her last-known state (R78) and takes that unit with §6.3's Steal while it is a Unit acting on the field, placed per R15. Nothing is taken when no unit destroyed her — a destroy effect, a Tribute or a Spell's damage leaves no killer (R42) — when the killer has since left the field (a mutual combat death) or lies dormant under a Stack (R13), or when her controller already controls it | #86, R13, R15, R42, R78 |
| R362 | Radiant Felinor Fiender counts twice | §8 #92's Radiant face: "Has twice the stats of all your Felinors, including those under Stack." R39 reads "has the stats of" as the printed stats plus the combined Felinor stats, and the patch changed no stats, so the Radiant face is its printed 10/14 plus twice that sum; the floor at 0 (R39) and R131's exclusion of itself apply to the sum before it is doubled | #92, R39, R116, R131 |
| R363 | Professor Curvature's "(4)+ Cost" | §8 #77: "Cry: (4)+ Cost cards cost (1) less on your next turn." A card qualifies when its cost is 4 or more at the point R65 applies Curvature, after every other modifier, so a 6 costs 5 (4 on the Radiant face) and a card another discount has taken below 4 is not reached. R48's timing is unchanged, and an X-cost card still ignores it (R65) | #77, R48, R65, R432 |
| R364 | Refresh | §8 #78: "Refresh 3 mana." Hearthstone's "Refresh Mana Crystals": current mana rises by up to X and never above max mana, and a player already at or above max — holding temporary mana (§2.3) — gains nothing. It is not temporary mana: paying 4 of 4 for /fullsend leaves 3, and paying 4 of 6 leaves 4, not 5 | #78, §2.3, §6.3 |
| R365 | Transmogulate replaces the hand too | §8 #83 now names the hand among its zones. The hand is one of R35's other zones: every card in it is replaced, one for one, by any Legendary of the pool, and ceases to exist (R35). The zones are walked board, hand, deck, graveyard, exile, which fixes the order of the rng draws. The opponent reads the hand as a count throughout and learns nothing of what it became (R177) | #83, R35, R177 |
| R366 | The words a card's text uses | Patch v0.1.1 asked for card text that is short, neatly formatted and consistent. A card's printed text calls the library the deck and a tribute a Tribute; writes a specific cost as the noun "(N) Cost" ("(4)+ Cost cards", "a (1) Cost or less card", "Discover a (2) Cost card") and a price as the verb "costs (N)" ("costs (1) less", "costs (0)"), never "Cost (N)", "N-cost" or "costing N", since patch v0.2.0 (R432); writes an embiggen card's bigger price as "Paid (4): …"; prints its keywords first, as one comma-separated list on a line of its own; starts each labelled ability ("Cry:", "Death:", "Start of turn:", "End of turn:", "Aura:", "Paid (4):", and since patch v0.2.0 the Activate labels "Activate:", "Activate 2:" and "Activate ♾️:", R384) on a line of its own, as the designer's own texts do; and writes every other line as sentences that start with a capital letter and end with a full stop. Patch v0.2.4 (issue #45) standardises terminology across all sets and retires variants via the vocabulary table: Deck (retires "library"), Tribute (retires "sacrifice"), "Return … to hand" (retires "Bounce"; Return names no owner — it always reaches the owner's hand — while Add keeps "Add … to your hand"), labelled turn triggers "Start of turn:" / "End of turn:" (retires "Start of your turn", "End of your turn", "At the start of your turn", "Activates at the start of your turn"), "(N) Cost" as noun and "costs (N)" as verb (retires "Cost (N)", "that costs (N)", "costing (N)"), "backrow" (retires "backrow zone"), "all" / "each" / "every" kept as written (no two faces resolve them the same way), standard casing "Start of game" and "Once per turn" (retires "Start of Game" and "Once per Turn"), and prose "costs (1) less" (retires "cost 1 less"). Glossary aliases for retired variants are emptied so exact term labels are used, retaining grammatical plurals (such as Plague Tokens) and specific card prompts (Look at your opponent's hand). Card text is presentation: no rule reads it (CLAUDE.md rule 7), §8's cells stay the mechanics in their own shorthand, and the engine's identifiers (`library`, `LIBRARY_CAP`, `sacrifice`) keep their names | §8, R277, R279, R384, R432 |
| R370 | A face-down trap shows its cost | v0.1.1 shows a face-down Trap's cost to both players, and the view carries it on the face-down marker as `cost` (§10.8), the number R351 fixes. The client draws that and nothing more (CLAUDE.md rule 7). A back in a backrow wears the cost as a gem where a face wears its cost, on both seats' boards, and is labelled "Face-down trap, (N) Cost"; a resting mouse or a long-press opens an overlay with the back, "Face-down trap", "(N) Cost" and that only the player who set it can see what it is, and draws no face. The showcase's back for a card the opponent set wears the cost its back shows where it landed, and the log's line for it ends ", (N) Cost", each read off the view's backrow while the card stands there face-down. A back the view gives no cost is drawn as before, and a back in a hand, which is no trap, shows nothing. A specific cost a player reads is written "(N) Cost", as card text writes one since patch v0.2.0 (R432) | §10.8, §10.10, R33, R97, R227, R351, R432 |
| R371 | Your own face-down trap says the other player can't see it | R33 lets a trap's controller read it while the other player sees a back, and the board drew it like any public card, so nothing said it was still hidden. `viewFor` marks the controller's view of a Trap or Field Trap that has not flipped face-up `unrevealed: true` (R351), from the same answer that gives the other player a back (`preview.isFaceDown`), so the mark and the back cannot disagree; it follows control as R33 does, and a Field Spell or a fired Field Trap never carries it. The client draws the face, since the controller reads it, under three marks that each read by shape, so none rests on colour: a dashed frame, a diagonal veil over the face that drifts slowly and holds still under reduced motion (R200), and a "Face down" tag with a struck-through eye, whose tooltip and hidden text say "Face down — your opponent can't see this card" and which keeps only the eye on a card too narrow for the word. Its hover preview and its sheet carry the same line over the face | §10.8, §10.10, R33, R200, R351 |
| R372 | Combo-Index shows its grade by its letter | #93's grade is a counter from 1 to 6 that the board drew as a bare number, while its text names grades E to S. The engine names the letter: `viewFor` carries `counters.gradeLetter` beside `counters.grade` on a backrow card, and #93's `preview` (R280) returns "Grade", with the grade as its value and its letter as `display`, and, below S, "N = the grades from E to the current one", with the plays the end of turn asks for, which is the number `gradeRises` compares the turn's plays with, so the value and the rise cannot disagree. A preview value may carry `display`, the word it prints as; a client prints it in the braces, "Grade {C}", and never derives one from the other. The preview is on the field only, since in hand the card has no grade and its text says it starts at E. The board's grade badge prints the letter ("C", titled "Grade C"); it sits on the compact backrow face, which prints no text, and the full face in hand and in the inspect overlays has no badge, so each shows the letter once, as R280 asks. The printed text is rewritten short (the grade, the rule, then a line per step, E to S), and Combo-Fodder's faces are a keyword line and an effect line. No behaviour changes | #93, #93.1, §8, §10.8, §10.9, §10.10, R27, R280 |
| R373 | Players read Deck and Tribute | v0.1.1: the rules' library is shown to players as the Deck, and Sacrifice as Tribute, wherever a player reads them outside card text, which is the catalog's: the deck pile ("Deck", "Your deck — 14 cards (order hidden)", "Deck full"), the log ("Your deck is empty: fatigue 3", "shuffled a card into the deck"), a prompt (#51's reveal), a Choose one's words (#30, #87), the glossary and the tutorial's coach. This spec keeps "library" and "Sacrifice" as its rules terms (§6), as the engine's identifiers (`library`, `LIBRARY_CAP`, `ownLibrary`, `libraryOverflow`) and the testids do. The glossary copies §6's Rule column verbatim and puts it into players' words as it builds, and a test over the client's sources refuses any string, template or JSX text with a space in it that says either old word | §3, §6, §10.10, R313, R318, #30, #51, #87 |
| R374 | The landing page deals a random hand | The designer asked for the homescreen's cards to be randomized. The landing page deals a fresh hand on each visit from every non-token Core card, read from the catalog: four cards of four different rarities, chosen at random and in a random order, the middle one on its Radiant face, then a card back, which is the fixed hand's shape. The deal is drawn once per visit and holds still while the page is up. Its randomness is the page's own (`Math.random`), which CLAUDE.md rule 4 does not bind outside the engine, the cards and the AI, and the source is injected, so a test deals the same hand every run | §9.2 |
| R375 | A card's patch history, first build | Issue #39 (2026-09-30) asked for card patches to be tracked, retroactively too, so older versions of cards can still be read (docs/classic-sets.md B4.2), and its first build landed on main beside patch v0.2.0's own (R388). When the two met, R388 and R507 kept the rule and the first build (`snapshots/`, `changes.json`, `src/history.ts` and the client's `History.tsx`) was removed, so this row now only fixes what both agreed on: the versions before v0.2.0 are, in order, v0.1.0 (the initial commit), v0.1.0b (the balance changes of issue #1), v0.1.0c (#95's text and The Coin), v0.1.0d (the Radiant pass, R275–R279) and v0.1.1, the first four labels given later to states git recorded (the three revisions were v0.1.0-r1 to -r3 until #85 gave a revision a letter, docs/issues-and-patches.md); the order of versions is `patches.json`'s, never a comparison of version strings; and the newest version's name is what every game record is filed under (R376) | §10.10, R105, R376, R388, R507 |
| R376 | Game records | The designer asked for card win rates backed by data (issue #48). Every finished game leaves one record: its match type (§9.5's mode, read off what made the match: `bo3` for a game of a Conquest series, else its room's mode or its queue tickets'; the match row is unchanged), its patch (the newest version in R388's `patches.json` of the build that played it, so a game is filed under the cards it was played with; since patch v0.2.0 that is also the catalog version, R388), each seat's pilot, `human` or `ai`, which seat took the first turn (§2.1's Player 1), each seat's decklist, its opening hand (its hand as the first turn begins: the cards it kept, its replacements and, for the seat going second, The Coin; empty when the game ended before a turn began), every card it drew into its hand after that (a card burned or cast on its draw never reached the hand), every card it played from its hand (a cast, §2.4 and R70, is not a play from hand; a play #96 My Pawn makes for the player it springs on is that player's; since patch v0.2.0, a play countered in its announce window was never played, §10.5 and R448, a play from a graveyard is not from the hand, and a play §10.5 step 3 replaced, C #23 Devil's Pact and R449, is the card it resolves as), and the winner, the reason and the turn count. The engine reads it off `(seed, decks, log)` by `replay.fold`'s fold (`summarizeGame`), so the game state and the log are unchanged, and a log that leaves the game without a result makes no record. The server files a live match under its match id, with two `human` pilots, once the result's transaction has committed, and never at the result's expense: a failure is logged and the result stands, a second write of the same match files nothing, and the reaper's ceiling draws (R112), whose actor stopped answering, file none. A record names no account, outlives the match's action log, and only the server reads or writes it. A practice game records nothing and sends nothing (R187), so it leaves no record | §9.5, §9.11, §10.5, R112, R187, R375, R448, R449 |
| R377 | Card win rates | A card's figures come from the records a query reads and, in them, the seats it counts, each seat's deck once per game: a game in which both decks held the card counts once for each, and a card is its catalog id, so a copy made in play counts as the card for a deck that held it, and a card no counted deck held has no figures. Its breakdowns are four: in deck (every such deck); in the opening hand; going first and going second (the deck's seat took the first turn, or did not); and played (the seat played it from its hand at least once) against its baseline, drawn but not played (the card reached the seat's hand, in its opening hand or by a later draw, and the seat never played it; a card neither played nor drawn is in neither). Each is a win rate, wins over games with a draw a game not won, as the AI's quality gates count them (§9.9), shown with its number of games beside it, and with no games it shows no rate. The played delta is the played win rate minus the drawn-but-not-played win rate, in percentage points, and shows nothing while either has no games. A query names any combination of match type (`bo1`, `bo3`, `random`, or every mode), patch (one, or every) and pilot (`human`, `ai`, or `unified`, every seat), and one card or all; `stats:cards` prints the four breakdowns for each card with its games beside every rate, or the same as JSON | §9.9, §9.11, R376, R378 |
| R378 | AI development runs | An internal AI development run (`pnpm ai:stats`) plays AI-against-AI games on the build in the checkout and files each finished one as a record of source `dev`, under the patch the run says it tests (the newest in `patches.json` unless it is told another), so a patch's run before it ships and its live games after compare by naming the patch in two queries, one of development data and one of live data. A development game is an All Random game (R258): both decks dealt by R258's deal from `${seed}:p1-deck` and `${seed}:p2-deck`, both seats AI on this spec's resources (no handicap, R180) at the browser's budget, its seeds a series of their own that no gate or tuning run plays. A card the shadow ban keeps out of the AI's own decks (R186) is dealt like any other, and the AI is known to play it badly, so its development figures say more about the AI than about the card. Live data is the default and development data is never mixed in unless asked for: a query reads `live` unless it names `dev` or `all`, the source is kept beside every record, a development record's id is `dev:`, its patch and its game's seed (`dev:v0.2.5:dev:7`) where a live one's is its match id, so neither can take the other's place and the same seeds run for another patch are other records, and `stats:import`, the one way a run's file enters the database, refuses a file holding any record but a development one, writing none of it, and skips a game already imported | §9.9, §9.11, R180, R186, R258, R376, R377 |
| R380 | One format; a pool that names no set draws from every set | Core, Classic and Classic+ are one format: a deck may mix all three under §2.6's rules (20 cards, no duplicate ids, no Tokens), and a Conquest trio needs 60 distinct cards, now from 268 (R251, R253). Nothing asks for a format per set, and the patch history keeps a later one that pins a version possible (R388). A random pool whose card names no set draws from every set, which is Hearthstone's reading of "a random card" and the only one that lets Core's generators see the new sets: #7, #54, #59, #67 (whose base pool gains C+ #22 Blood Moon, the one new (1) Cost Trap), #57 (whose KY pool gains C+ #41, C+ #42 and C+ #62), #83 (R35), #95 (its random Units, cards, Field Spells and Traps, and "cast a random Call to Chaos", R28), #98's Discover and Stitching (R352) and #99 reach every set, and so do the AI's decks and determinizations (R184, R185). A card that names a set keeps to it: #82 KY's Trial and #97 Zephyrs stay Core, C+ #73 takes Classic cards, C+ #73.1 a Classic or Classic+ Unit, and C+ #27 and C+ #75.1 Classic and Classic+ cards. A card's `index` is unique only within its set, so every lookup by index names the set or goes by id (§5); a Token is still in no pool its card does not name (§5.1) but R382's; and a token's printed rarity is display only, never a pool. The tutorial's fixed decks do not change (R291) | #7, #54, #57, #59, #67, #82, #83, #95, #97, #98, #99, C+ #27, C+ #73, C+ #73.1, C+ #75.1; §2.6, §5, §5.1, R184, R185, R251, R291 |
| R381 | Name collisions and rules-word names; Book of Wildfire, Grand Counterspell | No two cards share a name. C #16 is the Book of Flame, the one C #23 Devil's Pact and C #29 Book of Vital Kill name; C #55, which the designer's list printed as a second Book of Flame, is Book of Wildfire, a (1) Spell, Book, Epic: "Deal 4 damage.", Radiant "Deal 8 damage.", to a target, as C #16 — a name of its own, so nothing names it by accident. C #17 keeps Counterspell and C #72 is Grand Counterspell; no card names either. Cards named like a rules word keep their names — C #10 Exile, C #36 Burn, C #57 Echo (also the keyword Echo X) and C #30 Recycle (also inside C #64 Malzahar's Recycler) — and R279's proof keeps a named list of rules words that it never reads as a reference to such a card unless the card's `refs` lists it, so "Exile" in twelve Core texts and #79's "Echo 1" are not references to C #10 or C #57, and `refs` stay curated. The Classic+ cards spelled "Jlockheed" keep the designer's spelling under the one Jlockeed tag (R278). Classic+ numbers the designer wrote twice move: Jogg's Box is C+ #47, Brother Lar C+ #76 and his Brother Ping C+ #76.1, Anti-Softlock C+ #77 and Claude's Datacenter C+ #78, and the two tokens the list numbered as cards take the number of the card that makes them, Felinor Flagbearer Prime C+ #46.1 and the J-lease J-Jungle EX-plorer Pack C+ #75.1 | C #10, C #16, C #17, C #23, C #29, C #30, C #36, C #55, C #57, C #64, C #72, C+ #46.1, C+ #47, C+ #48, C+ #51, C+ #52, C+ #75.1, C+ #76, C+ #76.1, C+ #77, C+ #78; R278, R279 |
| R382 | Fruit pools include the five Grapes; Dropshipping's pool takes every token | A Fruit pool — a card that makes random Fruit — is the non-token Fruit cards of every set plus the five Grapes, C+ #65.1 to C+ #65.5, Tokens the designer made to be generated by any Fruit card; each definition in it is one entry, a Grape weighing as much as any card (only C+ #65 and C+ #66 roll a Grape by `GRAPE_ODDS`), and the card that makes the pool is still left out of it (R387). The Grapes are in no other pool but one: C+ #23 Dropshipping's "(including tokens)" is the designer's own exception to every token-free pool, and it draws from every card and token of every set but Dropshipping itself (R387) — a Grape, a Loser (R411), an AI card, The Coin (R245) — each at its printed cost (the Radiant face makes it cost (1)), repeats allowed (R60); a unit-token card it hands out sits in the hand as R11 allows | C+ #23, C+ #58, C+ #63, C+ #65, C+ #65.1–C+ #65.5, C+ #66, C+ #68, C+ #73; §5.1, R11, R60, R245, R387 |
| R383 | Animated | Animated is a keyword a Field Spell, Trap or Field Trap may print together with attack and health, its unit face; "Animated on your turn" is its variant (§6.1). To animate is to move the card from its backrow zone to a unit zone of its controller's — the one in its lane when that is open, else the leftmost open, unlocked, unreserved one (R64) — in Attack Position unless its text says otherwise (C #5 Tesla: Defense); with no such zone it does not animate and stays where it is, and a Trap that has fired then stays face-up in its zone, as a Field Trap does. An Animated Trap or Field Trap animates as the last step of its firing, and one already a Unit when it fires again neither moves nor changes position; an Animated Field Spell animates as it enters the field; an "Animated on your turn" card animates at the start of its controller's turn, after the mana refresh and the Brittle tick and before start-of-turn delayed effects and triggers (§2.2), and as it enters the field on its controller's turn, and it returns to its backrow zone at the end of its controller's cleanup, after every end-of-turn step, so its own end-of-turn text runs while it is a Unit. While animated it is a Unit for every rule: it attacks and is attacked, takes damage, counts among "your Units", is hit by "all Units" effects, is face-up, and keeps all of its text (a Field Trap's trigger still fires, an aura still applies); when it dies it goes to its owner's graveyard and fires Death. Moving between the two zones is not leaving the field: it keeps its damage, buffs, counters, memory and granted keywords (R78 does not apply), but entering the unit zone is entering it on that turn, so it is summoning sick (R83, R171). While an "on your turn" card is animated its backrow zone is reserved for its return, as a dying Reborn unit's is (R64). At a cleanup it does not return, and stays a Unit until its next one, when that zone has been Locked since (as R175 says of Reborn) or when it lies dormant under a Stack; one whose controller has changed has no home on its new side, and returns to its new controller's leftmost open backrow zone, or stays a Unit when there is none. In the backrow it is not a Unit: it cannot attack or be attacked, "all Units" effects skip it, and backrow effects reach it. A face-down Animated Trap is hidden like any trap until it fires (R33). The moves are reported by `animated` and `deanimated` (§10.3) | C #5, C #38, C+ #12.8; §2.2, §3.2, §6.1, R33, R64, R78, R83, R171, R175 |
| R384 | Activate | A card that prints "Activate: …" may use that ability once per turn, "Activate X: …" up to X times, and "Activate ♾️: …" any number of times, bounded by `ACTIVATE_UNLIMITED_CAP` (100) per card per turn so a game still ends. Only its controller activates it, in their own main phase with no prompt open and the game not over, while the card is on the field and acting — the top of its pile or a face-up backrow card; summoning sickness and exertion do not apply, since activating is not attacking. Uses are counted per card per turn on the instance (`memory.activations`), and leaving the field resets them (R78), so a card bounced and played again starts afresh, as a copy does. A cost written into the ability ("Tribute a Unit.", "Discard a random card.", a mana price) is paid as it is activated, an ability whose cost cannot be paid cannot be activated, and everything after the cost is the effect. The targets and modes the ability declares travel in the action, `activate { instanceId, ability?, targets?, modes?, tributes? }`, as a play's do (R81, R90), a Tribute cost's paying unit travelling in `tributes` as a play's does (R101), `ability` naming which one when a card has several, and choices made while it resolves are prompts. Activating is not a play: it counts for nothing that counts plays (Combo, Quickstriker, Ceaseless Void, "whenever you play a card"), though what its effect plays or casts counts as usual (R70). `legalActions` lists `activate` from the same check that refuses it, as it lists #98's power (R43), and `activatePower` is kept as its alias so old logs replay; patch v0.2.1 moves #98 onto Activate. Degrade and Upgrade move an Activate X by 1, never below 1, and Upgrade turns "Activate" into "Activate 2"; neither touches ♾️ (R386). An activation is reported by `activated { instanceId, ability }` | C #7, C #15, C #20, C #21, C #23, C #42, C #78, C #81, C #84, C+ #76.1, #98; §2.2, §6.2, §10.2, R43, R70, R78, R81, R90, R386 |
| R385 | Brittle X | Brittle N is a count on a card instance, kept in every zone the card passes through, hand to field included, as `radiant` and `costMod` are (R78), but ticking on the field only (R638). A count an effect gives is held until the card enters the field, and a printed Brittle starts when the card enters the field. At the start of its controller's turn, as a step right after the mana refresh (§2.2), the count of each card they control on the field drops by 1, but only once the card has had a full turn cycle with it: a count started on turn t (R638: the turn the card entered the field) first ticks at the first start of its controller's turn numbered t + 2 or later, and then at each start of its controller's turn, so a card that enters the field on its controller's turn 5 with Brittle 2 ticks to 1 at the start of turn 7 and crumbles at the start of turn 9. At 0 the card is destroyed: an ordinary destroy, which Indestructible ignores (R46), the count staying at 0 and checked again at each tick. "Give Brittle N" sets the count to N and "gain +N Brittle" adds N; Degrade lowers it by 1 and Upgrade raises it by 1 (R386); a Vanilla removes a printed Brittle and keeps a given one (§10.4); a copy never inherits a count (R57). The count is public on the field, except on a face-down card, whose count only its controller reads until it is revealed (C+ #74, R33), and shown to its owner in a hand; a card crumbling is reported by `crumbled { instanceId, zone }`, and the count's changes by `counterChanged` of kind `brittle`, which the other player reads only where they may read the count | C+ #23, C+ #74, T-AI-3; §2.2, §6.1, §6.3, R11, R33, R46, R57, R78, R386, R638 |
| R386 | Degrade and Upgrade | A Degrade applies one change to a card, drawn uniformly with the match rng from the changes that can apply to it now, and an Upgrade one from the mirror list; "Degrade a card 5 times" is five draws, and a card no change can reach is left alone with no draw (R129). An Immutable card is never changed (no draw); otherwise either reaches a card in any zone its text names — the field, a hand, a deck. The changes, Degrade then Upgrade: its cost 1 more, never above (4), or 1 less, never below (0), for a card whose own cost (R65, out of play) is below 4 or at least 1, never an X-cost card; its stats −4 or +4 split by a roll of k from 0 to 4 as k attack and 4 − k health, attack floored at 0 and current health never below 1, what the floors refuse being lost, for a Unit or an Animated card; a keyword it has removed (never Immutable and never a harmful one: Can't attack, Cannot be in Defense Position, Brittle) or one it lacks added from R21's pool (a Unit only); one X 1 worse or better — an X-cost card's X as it resolves (never below 1) or a numbered keyword's number (Armor, Lucky, Echo, Activate, Brittle and Spell Damage, more being better; Tribute, less being better, never below 1); or one number the card declares one step worse or better. The changes are the card's: kept on the instance as `tuning` (the cost change as `costMod`) in every zone and through leaving the field (R78), kept by a copy (R57), summed by a Fuse (R102) and gone with a Transform. A card declares its tunable numbers per face in the catalog as `params` — each with its base and Radiant value, whether more or less is better, a step (1 up to 5, 2 from 6 to 12, a quarter above, unless the card says otherwise) and its bounds — writes them into its text as `{key}`, and its script reads `param(ctx, key)`; an amount never drops below 1, and a threshold moves toward harder for a Degrade. The view carries the current values, which a client fills in (R280), and R277's mark compares the two faces with their values filled in. On the field the stats change moves max health; in a hand or a deck it changes the face the card enters with. A changed card shows its current cost, stats, keywords and numbers wherever its viewer may read it, and its owner does not see a change made inside a deck until the card leaves it (R311); `degraded` and `upgraded { instanceId, change }` are hidden like any other hand or deck event. C+ #41 KY's Constant reads "a number on a card" the same way: its cost (not X), attack, health, a numbered keyword's value or a declared number | C+ #8, C+ #41, C+ #69, C+ #70, C+ #71, C+ #72, C+ #73, T-AI-10; §6.3, R21, R57, R65, R78, R102, R129, R277, R280, R311 |
| R387 | A card never generates itself | A card never Discovers or makes a random copy of itself unless its text names a pool that holds it. The exclusion is by definition id, not index, since an index repeats across sets (§5), and it holds on every path by which a card makes a card from a pool: added to a hand, shuffled in, summoned, Discovered, cast (C+ #47 Jogg's Box, C+ #38.1 Solarius-Prime), transformed into (C+ #73.1), fused in (C+ #31 Fusion Lab, C+ #43 AI Slop, C+ #73's deck fusion) or put in another card's place; a fused card excludes every ingredient's definition. Only the Call to Chaos cards name such a pool — "cast a random Call to Chaos" (R28) and C+ #73's "replace your deck with random Call to Chaos" — so every other generator leaves itself out: C+ #54 Book of Books makes no Book of Books, C+ #58 Fruit Basket no Fruit Basket, C+ #23 Dropshipping no Dropshipping (R382), C+ #47 Jogg's Box never casts itself, and C+ #27 Zephrys Zealotism never offers itself. Copies are not generation: "summon a copy of this" (#12), "fill your board with copies of this" (C+ #46.1), Echo repeats and C+ #14 Forever&'s return are unaffected. A test sweeps every generating script with a seeded rng and asserts that its own id never comes out | #12, C+ #14, C+ #23, C+ #27, C+ #31, C+ #38.1, C+ #43, C+ #46.1, C+ #47, C+ #54, C+ #58, C+ #73, C+ #73.1; §5, §5.1, R28, R382 |
| R388 | Card patch history; the catalog version is the patch | Card patches are tracked, back to the first catalog: `packages/cards/patches/patches.json` lists every patch in order (version, date, title, source, notes), each patch's snapshot is the whole catalog as that patch left it, and a generated index maps each card id to the versions in which it changed. The history before this patch is rebuilt from the catalog's git history: v0.1.0 (the first Core catalog, 2026-09-18), v0.1.0b (the Core balance changes of 2026-09-22), v0.1.0c (#95's text and The Coin, 2026-09-24), v0.1.0d (the Radiant pass, R275–R279, 2026-09-25), v0.1.1 (patch v0.1.1, 2026-09-27) and v0.2.0 (this patch). The catalog version is the latest patch's version wherever it lives — the cards package, the server's env and the client's copy, and the `cards` rows and settings the catalog seed stamps — and a patch bumps it everywhere at once, so every patch reseeds (R105); patches are ordered by `patches.json`, never by comparing versions. A patch is made by one script that snapshots the catalog, diffs it against the previous snapshot and writes its notes card by card, and a test holds the catalog equal to the latest snapshot and the version equal to its version. An older version can be read: the collection's card detail shows each patch the card changed in, with its faces as they were and the words and numbers that changed marked as R277 marks a Radiant face; a public Patch notes page lists every patch and the cards it touched; the server serves any snapshot (`GET /api/catalog/:version`); and the engine can register an older snapshot, so a match folds under the version it started with. A patch that changes what a script does, not only a number, keeps the old script under `<id>@<version>`, so a match pinned to that version runs the old code. A card's `loc` (§5) is card data and part of its patch history | Every card; §5, §9.4, R105, R277 |
| R389 | Turn cap 60, ceiling 120 minutes | The game is a draw at the end of the 60th player-turn, 30 each (`TURN_CAP_PLAYER_TURNS`, §2.5, R2), twice the 30 before this patch, as the designer asked; the other limit in the game, the 75-second turn clock (R79), is unchanged. One draw a turn empties a 20-card deck on the first player's 17th turn and the second player's 16th, and fatigue (R3) then kills a hero at 30 health on the eighth empty draw, so two decks that do nothing end by fatigue at player-turn 48: the cap is now a backstop, reached only by games that heal, gain Armor, refill a deck (C #30) or replace fatigue (#75), and fatigue is the usual end of a long game. The hard match ceiling rises from 60 to 120 minutes (`MATCH_CEILING_MINUTES`, R79), since 60 player-turns at a full clock take 75. The AI reads the constant (§9.9), and every fuzz game still ends within the cap | Turn cap, fatigue, #75, C #30; §2.5, §9.9, R2, R3, R79 |
| R390 | The two-pass shadow-ban sweep | R186's sweep runs in two passes, so the AI is less inclined to ban a card and deals the cards on track to be banned more often. Pass 1 is the sweep as it was, over every non-token card of every set. A card is at risk when a pass's numbers meet a flag's condition at half strength — affordable on `minAffordableTurns` turns and played at most once, or an average evaluation change below half of `selfHarmDelta` — or when it is on the current ban or on `SHADOW_WATCH`, the cards an earlier sweep found at risk and cleared, which the next sweep counts as at risk from the start. Pass 2 sweeps only the at-risk cards, `seedsPerCardAtRisk` (24) games each per tier, and in every pass-2 game the AI's filler draw multiplies at-risk cards' weights by `atRiskBoost` (4), as `buildAiDeck`'s theme boost does, so an at-risk card is dealt as the forced card of its own games and as filler in everyone else's, and its numbers add up over every game it was dealt in. A `neverPlayed` ban needs pass-2 evidence at that tier — 6 affordable turns (not 3) and no play in any pass-2 game — and a `selfHarm` ban 8 plays (not 4); `error` and `timeout` ban as before, being bugs and not judgement. Pass 2's filler lifts the ban for at-risk cards banned `neverPlayed` or `selfHarm` and keeps out cards banned `error` or `timeout`, so a known bug is never filler; an `error` or `timeout` bans only its game's forced card, is listed against any at-risk filler in that game as a `suspect`, and bans that filler only if its own forced games repeat it. Pass 1's at-risk list is a pure function of pass 1's results and pass 2's seeds are named, so slices still run in parallel and a sweep of record replays. Rewarding the AI's search for playing at-risk cards was rejected: it would measure a different AI from the one that plays. The ban's scope is unchanged (R186) | §9.9, R186 |
| R391 | A Tribute can pay for its own zone | With its unit row full a Tribute card may still be played, into a zone its own Tribute empties: a unit zone whose pile is exactly one tributed unit (tributing the top of a pile frees nothing, since the card beneath resumes, R13), whose tributed unit has no Reborn (its zone would be reserved for the return, R64), and which is not Locked. `legalActions` pairs each zone with the paying sets that leave it open, and the play's check reads the pair (R90, R101); §10.5 already pays at step 2 and places at step 4, holding the named zone between (R210), so only the check changes, and a play reads its own side once the Tribute is paid as R360 reads the opponent's. It holds for a backrow card with a Tribute cost the same way, and never for an activation's Tribute cost, which needs no zone (R384) | #55, #66, C #45, C #61, C #80; §3.2, §10.5, R13, R64, R90, R101, R210, R360, R384 |
| R392 | C #15 Nose Hunter | C #15's "Discard a random card: Exile the bottom card of each opponent's deck" is an Activate ability used once per turn (R384): "cost: effect" is how a card is clicked to do an effect, and the random discard is its cost, so with an empty hand it cannot be activated. "Each opponent" is the multiplayer phrasing Heroic Power uses and means the opponent. The Radiant face also exiles a random card from the opponent's hand | C #15; R384 |
| R393 | C #28 Second Wind | C #28's Cry exiles its controller's deck and then discards their hand, and the discards land in the graveyard before its Aura begins, so the discarded hand is playable from there — the reading that gives the card its name. While it stands its controller may play cards of every type from their graveyard (§6.3 Play), and on the base face their cards that would go to their graveyard are exiled instead (a replacement, §6.2); the Radiant face drops the replacement and allows only a play that would pay at least (1), which stops a (0) loop. With no deck left every draw is fatigue (§2.4, R3) | C #28; §2.4, §6.2, §6.3, R3 |
| R394 | C #33 Joro | C #33 answers from its owner's hand only: when the opponent targets one of its owner's Units — an attack's target declared at §4.2 step 2, before the trap window, or a pick an enemy play, cast, activation or prompt answer makes — Joro is summoned into its owner's leftmost open unit zone (with none, nothing happens), with no Cry and summoning sick, and the attack or the pick is redirected to it (Redirect, §6.3), a replacement at "a friendly unit is targeted" (§6.2). "Targeted" means chosen: a random pick or an "all" effect targets nothing. One Joro answers one targeting, and a play that names several of its owner's Units redirects the first. The Radiant face adds Indestructible and keeps 1/1, a named exception to R275's stat standard | C #33; §4.2, §6.2, §6.3, R275 |
| R395 | C #38 Jackiestan Auctioneer | While C #38 is face-down only its activation condition is live: it fires when any player plays their 3rd card in a turn (their 2nd on the Radiant face; casts count, R70) and then animates (R383). Its "whenever a player plays a card" text works from the play after the one that set it off, as a permanent never answers its own arrival (R119), and goes on working animated or, for want of a unit zone, face-up in the backrow: on every card either player plays, its controller draws 1 and deals 2 damage (4 on the Radiant face) to the enemy hero | C #38; R70, R119, R383 |
| R396 | An X card on the field costs its X | An X-cost card on the field costs the X it was played for (the instance's `x`, which §2.3 stores) wherever a rule compares or counts costs; anywhere else, and on the field when it arrived with no X chosen (a Recruit, a summon), it costs 0, as R65 says. This amends R65's "outside play … comparisons", which would otherwise read a field card at 0, so C #25 Lag in the System does not exile a C+ #69 Buff Billy played for 3; C #10, C #18, C #25, C #32 and C #39 read costs this way | C #10, C #18, C #25, C #32, C #39, C #87, C+ #69; §2.3, R65 |
| R397 | C #48 Hired Shrimp | C #48's base Cry may target any permanent, and at resolution destroys it only if its card's `loc` (§5) is greater than Hired Shrimp's, so a wrong guess fizzles: the joke is kept as a rule. `loc` is public, printed in the inspect overlay, so the guess can be informed. The Radiant face offers only the permanents that qualify, which is its text's "valid targets are highlighted", except a face-down card its chooser may not read, which is always offered and judged at resolution as on the base face, so the offer never gives away that card's `loc` (R177, §10.8). A played Unit's Cry is chosen with the card still in hand, so Hired Shrimp never offers itself (R70, R90) | C #48; §5, §10.8, R70, R90, R177 |
| R398 | C #50 Voidwalker | C #50's Aura exiles the cards that would go to a graveyard (on its Radiant face, the opponent's cards only) while it is on the field, a replacement (§6.2). Its own card reaches the graveyard when it dies: it has left the field, and its aura with it, as its move begins | C #50; §6.2 |
| R399 | C #57 Echo | C #57 has the text of the last Spell either player played (a cast is a play, R70): the game records it by definition and face (§10.1), in hand the view carries that Spell's face under Echo's name, as it carries a fused card's (R243), and played, Echo declares and resolves that Spell's choices and script. A played Echo records the Spell it copied, never Echo, so it cannot copy itself into a loop; with no Spell played yet it has no text and does nothing. "Spell" is the Spell type, not a Field Spell. The Radiant face adds Echo 1 (§6.2) | C #57; §6.2, §10.1, R70, R243 |
| R400 | C #62 Living Bomb | C #62's "they destroy all cards with a Plague Counter" means each player's own: at the start of each player's turn, the permanents that player controls with a Plague Token on them are destroyed (on the Radiant face, only at the start of the opponent's turn), the only reading under which the Radiant face is the stronger. "Plague Counter" is the Plague Token (§6.3), and both players' turns take R68's order | C #62; §6.3, R68 |
| R401 | C #66 EU Striker | Neither of C #66's triggers answers the play that moved it (R119): a Unit its owner plays summons it from their hand (after that Unit resolves; no Cry, summoning sick, leftmost open zone) and does not bounce it, and each later card its owner plays returns it to their hand (R78), the card that bounces it not summoning it back | C #66; §6.2, R78, R119 |
| R402 | C #78 Mutate Spell | C #78 is a Field Spell (the designer wrote Spell), since an Activate ability needs a card that stays on the field, as §5.3 corrected #68's type. Its Activate ♾️ (R384) removes a Plague Token from a permanent, a target declared with the activation; then an enemy permanent is exiled, a backrow card of its controller's draws them 2 (4 on the Radiant face), and a Unit of theirs makes a forced attack on a random enemy, hero or unit (R53; twice on the Radiant face). The Radiant face fuses the enemy permanent onto a card of its controller's of that type which they choose — on the field, in their hand or in their deck, the deck's options shown to them only (§10.8) — the enemy card ceasing to exist (R77), or exiles it when there is none | C #78, #68; §5.3, §10.8, R53, R77, R384 |
| R403 | C #88 Siphon Squad | A Trap or Field Trap whose text has no activation condition is live while face-down: C #88's aura works from the moment it is set, and it stays face-down until something reveals it. The opponent sees their attack drop and may deduce the card; R33 hides a card's identity, not its consequences. Its self-Tribute is a condition checked at every state check, the one right after it is set included, and the Radiant face's "0 Attack" sets attack last, after every other layer (§10.4) | C #88; §10.4, R33 |
| R404 | C #90 In Too Deep | C #90's quests count from the moment each opens, on either player's turn, and completion is noticed at the state check after the event that completes it, when the card's controller chooses a reward (their prompt, even on the other player's turn), which sets the next quest: 1, draw 2 cards (rewards A or B); 2, two enemy permanents destroyed, by anything (C or D); 3, control 3 or more permanents at once (D or E); 4, end a turn with 3 or more mana unspent (F or G); 5, your cards deal 12 damage to enemies in total (G or H); 6, your Units have 10 or more total attack and 10 or more total health at once (H or I); 7, end a turn with 5 or more mana unspent (J); 8, three cards enter either exile (K); 9, a draw of yours takes the last card of your deck, one already empty when the quest opens completing it at once, and never a card leaving it any other way (L); 10, six or more Units in your graveyard (M). A leads to quest 2, B to 3, C to 4, D to 5, E to 6, F to 7, G to 8, H to 9 and I to 10, and J to M end a line: A heal your hero 6; B deal 3 damage to a target; C return 2 random cards from your graveyard to your hand; D place 3 Plague Tokens; E a random Unit of yours gets +3/+3; F bounce a target permanent; G your opponent discards 2, their choice; H draw 2; I Recruit a card; J gain 100 mana; K exile your opponent's deck; L Aura: you may play cards from your graveyard (§6.3 Play); M Aura: your Units have Indestructible — the two auras holding while In Too Deep is on the field. The quest state is on the instance (§10.1), and the view shows the open quests with their progress and the rewards on offer. The Radiant face grants every reward a completed quest offers and opens every quest they lead to; a quest reached by two paths opens once, and a reward two completed quests offer is granted by each | C #90; §2.3, §6.3, §10.1, §10.6 |
| R405 | C+ #2 Groom Shroom | C+ #2's "Felinors" are Felinor-tagged Units, the creatures (a Spell cannot fill a board), here and on C+ #30 Felinor Fuser: it fills its controller's empty, unlocked unit zones with random non-token Felinor Units of any set (R64, R380), repeats allowed (R60), with no Cry, and grants each Taunt (on the Radiant face they are Radiant). The declared attack that set it off still hits its controller's hero: the Taunts arrived after §4.2 step 3, as Hearthstone orders it | C+ #2, C+ #30; §4.2, R60, R64, R380 |
| R406 | C+ #7 The House | C+ #7's "Right-House Protector" is #3 Right-house defender, the twin of C+ #6 Wrong-House Attacker, and ⅔ and ⅓ are the odds: its Cry and each start of its controller's turn summon a Right-house defender with chance 2 in 3, else a Wrong-House Attacker (the Radiant face summons both), generated from the catalog and summoned for its controller with no Cry; being cards and not tokens, they go to the graveyard when they die | C+ #7, C+ #6, #3 |
| R407 | C+ #9 Silence | A Vanilla on a backrow card (C+ #9's Radiant face reaches any permanent) leaves it with no text: a Field Spell's aura stops, a face-down trap can never fire and sits in its zone as an inert card, and an Animated card loses Animated where it stands (R383). As on a unit, granted keywords stay (§10.4), and an Immutable card is untouched (R23) | C+ #9; §6.3, §10.4, R23, R383 |
| R408 | C+ #12 The Mother Pancake (a Field Spell's unlabelled text is its Cry) | A Field Spell's unlabelled one-time text is its Cry: C+ #12.6 Frozen Wastes and C+ #12.7 Legion of the Hungry resolve their text as a Cry when played and then stay in the backrow with no further text, a body for backrow effects to hit. Frozen Wastes' "a card" is the top card of the deck, as Hearthstone's Doom Pact takes it, and "each one" counts the Units that died, not an Indestructible survivor; Legion's five cards are five different random ones (R60), and the Units among them are summoned out of exile with no Cry until the board is full, the rest staying exiled | C+ #12, C+ #12.6, C+ #12.7; §6.2, R60 |
| R409 | C+ #12 The Mother Pancake (resummon makes copies) | C+ #12.8 Frostspatula's "Resummon all Units destroyed by this" makes copies, as Hearthstone's Frostmourne does, and the originals stay in their owners' graveyards: it remembers each Unit it killed (R42) by definition and face, and its Death — as a Unit, or destroyed in the backrow, since Death is leaving the field for a graveyard (§6.2) — summons a fresh copy of each for its controller, tokens included, until the board is full (on the Radiant face, made Radiant) | C+ #12.8; §6.2, R42, R383 |
| R410 | C+ #14 Forever& | C+ #14's "When this leaves your hand, add it right back to your hand" is read as "After this resolves, return it to your hand": the next Spell its player plays (a player modifier that waits until used, not only this turn) gains an enchantment that rides the card in every zone (§10.1), so after each resolution it returns to its owner's hand, and it can't cost less than (2) ((1) on the Radiant face, which also draws 1), a floor applied after every discount (R65). A discarded or countered copy does not come back: that would make it undiscardable and uncounterable, and the floor shows the aim was a repeatable Spell, not an indestructible one | C+ #14; §10.1, R65, R78 |
| R411 | C+ #19 League of Losers: Mid Loser's Cry fires | A summon that names the card it summons fires that card's Cry (R1): C+ #19.3 Mid Loser's Cry fires when C+ #19 League of Losers summons it. That is its only way onto the field but one — C+ #23 Dropshipping can hand a Loser out as a card, which fires its Cry when played from hand like any card — so under R1 alone its Cry would be nearly dead text. League of Losers summons its five into its controller's unit zones 1 to 5 in that order, skipping an occupied or Locked zone (§3.2) | C+ #19, C+ #19.3, C+ #23; §3.2, R1 |
| R412 | C+ #19 League of Losers: Tranquility and Berserk | C+ #19.5 Bot Loser's Radiant "Tranquility makes this unit not be able to go Berserk" is the designer's name for the immunity: "This can't go Berserk." Berserk (§6.1) is a flag C+ #19.2 Jungle Loser sets, lost when the unit leaves the field; while it is set, at its controller's start and end of turn the unit makes a forced attack on its own controller's hero (§4.2, R53), which never strikes back. Jungle Loser's "destroys" is R42's killer, and its Radiant face credits the kill to Bot Loser, firing Bot Loser's "whenever this destroys a Unit" as if it had made the kill | C+ #19.2, C+ #19.5; §4.2, §6.1, R42, R53 |
| R413 | C+ #22 Blood Moon | C+ #22's "is healed" is read as "would be healed" (a replacement, §6.2), so the heal that sets it off is converted too; otherwise the first heal would always escape it.
